                                    61
               Part II The World of the Text


apostles, so why not make SBJohn apostolic as well? Such
a theory, attractive as it is, may assume that the author(s)
of SBJohn were on the defensive, only responding to
the tendencies of “proto-orthodox” interpreters. It is
probably best to see the author(s) of SBJohn as part of
a larger trend in the early Christian world. “Apostolic”
Christianity was everywhere, and apostles were not yet the
property of a particular group. The author(s) of SBJohn
claimed that their sacred text was apostolic because they
believed in sacred revelation through apostles.
   To be sure, no scholar vouches for SBJohn being writ-
ten by a historical “John son of Zebedee.” This John is
a fictional character, whose depth and personality only
begins to flourish in late second-century works like the
Acts of John.
   At the same time, some scholars – evidently because
GJohn is in the canon – do in fact argue that it was writ-
ten (or somehow based on the memory) of a flesh-and-
blood fisherman called the son of Zebedee, sometimes
identified with the beloved disciple.19 Probably, however,
the beloved disciple in GJohn is just as fictionalized as the
John in SBJohn. Even if these figures existed historically,
their literary function (the seal of authentication) was the
key. We do not know the actual author(s)/editor(s) of
either GJohn or SBJohn.


                      SBJohn as Prequel
It makes sense now how SBJohn could be viewed as a con-
tinuation of GJohn. It plucks a character from GJohn – a

                19
                     E.g., Bauckham 2017, 358–411.


                                62
                   3 The Apostolic Eagle


character with a putatively special relationship with Jesus –
and gives him a chance to pose philosophical and exegeti-
cal questions to the post-ascension Jesus. In brief, SBJohn
depends on the narrative and tradition involving John as
the author of GJohn, and that is why John son of Zebedee
reappears as the main narrator in SBJohn.
   Despite borrowing the character of John, however,
lengthy verbal and material connections shared by SBJohn
and GJohn are not abundant. SBJohn never explicitly
quotes GJohn, or invokes its side characters (for instance,
Nathaniel, Thomas, Mary), or brings up flashbacks from
GJohn. Perhaps the reason is obvious: SBJohn is not a
rereading of GJohn specifically, but of Genesis. In other
words, SBJohn is not a life of Jesus, but a far-reaching
revelation focused on God, the cosmos, and salvation. It
works to fill in the theological and cosmic backstory of
GJohn. In this sense, the revelation in SBJohn is a kind of
prequel to GJohn, filling in perceived gaps.
   At the same time, SBJohn is still “Johannine” in the
sense that there are structural similarities and allusions
linking SBJohn to GJohn. GJohn begins with a prologue
that invokes Genesis and the time before creation (“In the
beginning was the Logos,” 1.1). It mentions abstract fig-
ures like Logos, Light, Grace, Truth, and the Onlyborn.
This Onlyborn, identified with Christ, is the being through
whom all (true reality) is created (1.3). Christ plays this
role in SBJohn as well (7.16). In both SBJohn and GJohn,
Jesus appears as a descending redeemer revealing a an
often-mysterious wisdom in long monologues, with the
disciples posing occasional questions. Jesus sometimes
enters into controversy with the Pharisees, and sometimes
in the temple area. These Pharisees occasionally accuse

                             63
                   Part II The World of the Text


Jesus’ supporters of being duped (GJohn 7.12, 45–49).
The Jesus of GJohn prioritizes spirit over flesh and under-
scores that one must have Spirit to gain eternal life (6.63).
   There are some direct allusions in SBJohn to GJohn
as well. One of the more obvious is SBJohn 1.3, where
John says that Jesus “went back to the place from which
he came” (GJohn 16.5, 28; cf. 13.3). The Father who sent
Jesus (SBJohn 2.2) is an allusion to GJohn 3.17 (cf. 10.36;
18.37). In John’s initial vision, a light illumines “all cre-
ation” (SBJohn 3.1) just like the light enlightening every
person coming into the world (GJohn 1.9).20 In SBJohn
20.31, moreover, John asks virtually the same question
as Nicodemus does in GJohn 3.4 (about reentering the
mother’s womb). (Interestingly, Nicodemus was chided
for his question, but John is praised.21) ‘Do not let your
heart grow faint’ (3.7) recalls GJohn 14.1. Finally, the dis-
tinction between the spirit/paraclete (GJohn 16.12–13)
and a spirit of deceit (1 John 4.6), may have inspired the
true spirit (identified with Luminous Insight) and the
counterfeit spirit in SBJohn.22 All this material is mani-
festly Johannine, though transformed.
   If SBJohn stands in a Johannine trajectory, this does
not mean that it entirely agrees with GJohn. In fact,
SBJohn seems at points to openly disagree with GJohn.
For instance, SBJohn attributes no salvific significance
to Jesus’ death on the cross. (Indeed, Jesus’ revelation in
SBJohn has nothing to do with his death.) SBJohn shows
no antagonism to “the Jews” in general (it only mentions
a single hostile Pharisee). The beloved disciple in GJohn

20                     21
     King 2006, 235.        See further DuBois 2012, 108–117.
22
     King 2006, 236.


                                     64
                   3 The Apostolic Eagle


is a witness to Jesus, but not a recipient of special revela-
tion as he is in SBJohn. These are important differences.
   SBJohn is also not solely Johannine. Its author(s) prob-
ably used other gospels as well. John’s going to a deserted
mountain reminds one of Jesus’ temptation in the wil-
derness (GMark 1.12–13, par.), as well as his prayer in
deserted places (GLuke 5.16), including the mount of
Transfiguration (GMark 9.2–7; GLuke 9.28). Alistair
Logan proposed that the virgin Spirit Barbelo was
designed as the archetype of the obedient virgin Mary in
Luke, delighted with heavenly revelation and visitation,
who spiritually conceived the Onlyborn (GLuke 1.30–35,
46–49). He believed that the Son’s anointing, perfection,
and elevation are derived from Christian speculation on
the heavenly preexistent Son in Hebrews 1 and 2.23 Yet
one could also trace the spiritual anointing by the Father
back to Jesus’ baptism as recorded in the Synoptics
(GMatthew 3.13–17; GLuke 3.21–22).
   Just as GJohn presented a revelation deeper than what
could be found in the Synoptics, so SBJohn presented a
revelation deeper and more thorough than anything in
GJohn. The Gospel of John, for all its “spiritual” aura,
did not explain how the Savior was selected and sent, the
identity of his Father, and the nature of the higher realm
(SBJohn 2.2–3). To be sure, GJohn partially defines God
by calling him “Spirit” (4.24), and refers to heavenly
“dwellings” (14.2), but beyond such brief formulations,
the Father and his Fullness were left unknown (1.18).
   The author(s) of SBJohn stepped in to complete
the revelation. The Jesus of SBJohn is effectively the

                     23
                          Logan 1991, 53–54.


                                65
                   Part II The World of the Text


Paraclete promised in GJohn, offering further explana-
tions of deeper mysteries going all the way back to cre-
ation and even before it. Just as the Paraclete proclaims
what is to come (GJohn 16.14) and explains what the
earthly Jesus meant, so does Jesus in SBJohn. Philip’s
question in GJohn 14.8 is finally answered – “Show us
the Father.” SBJohn’s entire Father discourse is designed
to show him – and not only the Father, but all the events
and figures who unfolded “in the beginning;” and in that
beginning, the Logos was not the main character.


                 SBJohn and the Acts of John
The figure of the Beloved Disciple in John is still a
shadow of a character and he is not directly identified
with John son of Zebedee. To understand John as a char-
acter in SBJohn, it’s best to compare him with the late
second-century character of John in the Acts of John.
John in Acts of John is a sage, a healer, a man who per-
forms so many resurrections they are almost trivial. He is
also a mystagogue, a man who can perceive the truth of
Jesus through his many forms. This side of John comes
out most clearly in Acts of John 87–105.24 These chapters
form a coherent unit of text found in a single manuscript,
and this unit has frequently been characterized as “gnos-
tic,” just like SBJohn.25
   Acts of John 87–105 begins with John explaining to his
disciples how and why Jesus appears in multiple forms.26

24
     On Acts of John, see Culpepper 1994, 187–250; Klauck 2008, 15–46.
25
     Junod Kaestli 1983, 26–29.
26
     On polymorphy in Acts of John as distinguished from metamorphosis,
     see Lalleman 1995, 97–118; 1998, 170–72.


                                   66
                         3 The Apostolic Eagle


John says that this was how Jesus usually appeared to
him as well, even when he was embodied on earth. In
the course of John’s reminiscence, we learn that John has
a brother, James, which shows that we are dealing with
John son of Zebedee (Acts of John 88.4). This John is also
the beloved disciple, whom Jesus embraced when they
reclined at table (89.5).
   John proceeds to give examples of Jesus’ many forms.
When Jesus first called John, John recalled that for him
Jesus morphed from a handsome, cheerful young man
to an old, bald man with a thick beard (Acts of John
88.8–89.1). John later encountered Jesus on the Mount
of Transfiguration as someone “not like a man at all.” His
feet were whiter than snow (cf. Rev 1.15), and his head
stretched up to heaven (cf. GPeter 10.40). Accordingly,
John had abundant opportunity to reflect on Jesus’ “unity
within many faces” (Acts of John 91.3).
   It is impossible not to connect this episode with the
appearance of Jesus to John in SBJohn, where John won-
ders whether Jesus manifested a “[woman] of multiple
forms within the light revealed in mutual oscillation
or whether” Jesus “was one likeness with three faces”
(SBJohn 3.4). One might even suppose that John’s experi-
ence in SBJohn depends on the presentation of the poly-
morphic Jesus in Acts of John. After all, Jesus chides John,
“You are no stranger to this likeness” (SBJohn 3.6).27
   The John of the Acts of John is, in short, much like
his counterpart in SBJohn: He is the disciple and apostle
of Jesus who sees him in manifold forms and receives a

27
     Lalleman rightly observes that the “dual appearances” in Acts of John
     are different from the “threefoldness of the appearances” in SBJohn
     (1998, 137).


                                     67
               Part II The World of the Text


revelation. At the end of the revelation in Acts of John,
John shores up the faith of his fellow spirits: “You, there-
fore, beloved, must also be persuaded, that I exhort you
not to worship a human being, but God unchangeable,
God undefeatable, higher than all authority, all power,
older and stronger than all angels, all creatures, and all
eternities. If you hold fast to this and are built up in this,
you shall possess your soul indestructible” (104). Such a
Johannine mission and theology is consistent with what
we find in SBJohn’s story of God and salvation.
   In terms of implied authorship, we also observe similar
strategies in Acts of John 87–105 as we do in SBJohn.
Initially, an editorial introduction introduces John in the
third person (87). Then John speaks in the first person a
revelation which he shares with his fellow disciples. This
multipart revelation takes up most of the space of the nar-
rative. The implied reader is apparently meant to believe
that chapters 87–105 were either written by the son of
Zebedee or based on traditions that he spoke to disciples.
John’s point that he cannot put some of his experiences
into writing (88.2) assumes that he was a writer. In terms
of appealing to John’s authority as a writer and speaker,
the Acts of John and SBJohn are akin.


                        Conclusion
At the start of the second century, John son of Zebedee
was little more than a thinly developed disciple of Jesus
mentioned sporadically in the Synoptics and Gospel of
John. But after a century of Christian reflection on this
fisherman’s son, his status was raised considerably and his
character filled out. Thanks to the Gospel of John, he

                             68
                  3 The Apostolic Eagle


became the disciple whom Jesus loved the most. When
fused with John of Patmos, he was seen as the recipient
of a deeper and higher revelation in the region of Asia
Minor. His abundant miracles – including more than one
resurrection – were told in the Acts of John.
   The stage was set for the author of SBJohn to choose
this rising star among the apostles and feature him as
the recipient of a deeper and more philosophical reve-
lation – a veritable “theory of everything” comprising a
story of God, of the cosmos, and of salvation. In doing
so, the author(s) of SBJohn authenticated their revelation
and continued John’s story. At the same time, they told
what was “in the beginning,” a story which made sense of
the Savior who, sent by his Father, descended to save his
people. SBJohn reveals this Father, speaks of the nature
of the higher realm, and shows how this universe is an
imprint of that higher realm. In this way, John’s spiritual
gospel was perfected for a new generation of Christian
readers.




                            69
                                    4
                     Recreating Creation
                                   u


                            Introduction
The stories of SBJohn rewrite sections of Genesis – and
they do so twice: First for the upper world, then for the
lower.1 The twofold reading made sense because it was
widely recognized in antiquity that there was a double
creation roughly corresponding to Genesis 1, which
speaks of the upper, stable world, and Genesis 2–3, which
speaks of the lower, variable creation.2 When reading for
the upper world, then, the emphasis was on Genesis 1.
When reading for the lower world, more details were
pulled from Genesis 2–6.
   The author(s) of SBJohn did not hit every verse in
Genesis, but only select passages which served as light-
ning rods of interpretive interest. At times, they read the
very same verses to unveil truths about the higher and
lower worlds. They mixed direct quotations of Genesis
with more abundant allusions and echoes, which con-
stantly prod the reader to realize deeper mysteries.
   This chapter studies SBJohn’s deployment of Genesis
for both the higher and lower worlds, examines how
SBJohn explicitly corrects Genesis, and surveys SBJohn’s
use of biblical books outside of Genesis.

1
    King 2013, 3–22.
2
    Philo, Questions and Answers on Genesis 1.2; On the Creation of the
    World 16, 129–130. See further Runia 1986, 334–40.


                                   70
                    4 Recreating Creation


                   SBJohn and Genesis
SBJohn is neither a replication of Genesis nor a defense
of it; it is a critical revision that results in a new narrative.
In this narrative, Genesis’ creation story is not outright
rejected, but recreated. Rewriting is a form of interpre-
tation – perhaps the most dramatic form. It differs from
allegory, which preserves the base text. At the same time,
allegorical reading methods informed the rewriting of
SBJohn. Characters from Genesis, like God, spirit, the
primeval waters, Adam, Seth, and Seth’s seed, were seen
as signs and symbols of higher realities – higher realities
who became new characters in a new narrative.
   Why did the author(s) of SBJohn reformulate Genesis?
After all, they could well have discarded Hebrew scrip-
tures, turning to Orphic or Chaldean oracles, for instance,
or their own likely story, freshly created like the new
myths of Plato and Plutarch told to illustrate philosoph-
ical ideas. SBJohn’s introduction, at least, is a new narra-
tive, or a new iteration of gospel narrative. But the main
content of SBJohn adheres fairly closely to the structure
of Genesis 1–7.
   It would seem that these author(s) believed that Genesis
was the genuine revelation of the creator. But, in their
opinion, this lower creator was not the true God. As recip-
ients of Wisdom’s light power, the writer(s) of SBJohn
had greater knowledge than the creator. Therefore they
could retell the creation story more accurately and more
truthfully than the creator’s prophet Moses. As we shall
see, the author(s) of SBJohn did not simply use the text of
Genesis to rewrite the story of God, creation, and salva-
tion. They also used intervening Jewish traditions.


                               71
               Part II The World of the Text


                     The Upper World
The Invisible Spirit as ‘God and Father of all’ (SBJohn
4.3) is evidently to be identified with the ‘God’ who
exists in the Beginning according to Genesis 1.1. He may
derive the name ‘Spirit’ from Genesis 1.2, which speaks
of a Spirit over the waters. The primeval waters are inter-
preted as ‘light water’ (SBJohn 5.3), for light shone over
them (Gen 1.3).
   Out of these waters comes Barbelon, both primal light
and primal Human. Barbelon as the archetypal Human
is a reading of Genesis 1.26. Presumably the name
“Adamas” derives from this same verse as well. In the
Hebrew of 1.26 (compare 5.2), God makes ’adam – and
with a Greek ending, the term adamos could be heard as
adamas, the “indomitable one.”
   Barbelon as First Human is also an interpretation of
Gen 1.3 (LXX), the manifestation of the primal light
(φῶς). The manifestation of the primal light is also the rev-
elation of the primal man (φώς, with a different accent).3
This light man appears out of the waters of life, and since
the word “life” (ζωή) is feminine in Greek, Barbelon as
light and life is both male and female. She is, further-
more, “mother of all the living” (Gen 3.20).
   The anointed Christ as “God over all” who creates by
speech (SBJohn 7.16) would seem to come from the com-
mon refrain in Genesis 1 (“God said, ‘Let there be …’
and there was”; cf. Ps 32.9 LXX). Christ as the Light over
all is the first light of creation (Gen 1.3; GThomas 77).
God looks upon the Light and recognizes its goodness


                     3
                         Pagels 1999, 477–96.


                                72
                       4 Recreating Creation


(Gen 1.4) – a detail which explains the Father’s rejoicing
in SBJohn 7.5.
   The four Lights or archangels manifestly come from
Jewish lore, and specifically from reflection on the four
creatures beneath the throne of God in Ezekiel (1.4–21;
10.1–22) and Revelation (4.6–7) or the four archangels
variously named in 1 Enoch 9.1, 40.9, and 71.8. Yet their
ultimate exegetical origin may well be Genesis 1.14–17 –
the lights in the firmament. One of the archangelic names,
Oroiael = Uriel (literally “god of light”) was traditional.4
   The other three (Armozel, Daveithe, and Eleleth) are
distinctive. Armozel, if his name comes from the Greek
ἁρμάζω plus the Hebrew word for god, may mean “god
who fits, prepares, or engineers.” Daveithe may come
from “David” (as he appears in Irenaeus, AH 1.29) with
a Hebraizing ending. Eleleth sounds like the doubling of
’el (“god”) with a Hebrew suffix. Elsewhere in “Sethian”
literature he appears as a great angel and initiator of the
lower creation.5 Eleleth and Daveithe appear sporadi-
cally in Egyptian magical and homiletical literature as
benevolent (arch-)angels.6 Origen says that these names
came from “magic,” and small-scale ritual practitioners –
among them Jews – employed similar names.7
   Adamas, Seth, Seth’s seed, and the repenters (SBJohn
9.7–10) are, as it were, the Platonic Forms of the redeemed.
They emerge from a reading of Genesis 5, when Adam

fathers Seth as his true image, and then Seth’s genealogy
is related. The first four members of the genealogy repre-
sent the seed of Seth proper. The hinge in the genealogy
is Enoch, who was associated with repentance in Jewish
tradition.8 SBJohn probably depends on this tradition,
thus the repenters of the fourth light are implicitly the
children of Enoch.
   The generations from Enosh to Jared make up the seed
of Seth, while the generations from Enoch to Noah can
be called the “repenters” (See Figure 4.1).
   The figure of Wisdom is a kind of upper Eve who,
desiring to be like God (Gen 3.5), wanted to manifest her
likeness without a partner.9 The result is Yaldabaoth, a

malformed child, who recalls the cursed Cain who came
from Eve.
   The crowing of Yaldabaoth crowns his creative acts.
He swaggers over his angelic swarm who rule the planets
and the lower, material realms. In Genesis, the creator
“sees” his creation and declares it very good (Gen 1.31).
There may already be a hint of pride here, which the
author(s) of SBJohn press to the point of ridicule.


                          The Lower World
After Yaldabaoth’s boast, the author(s) of SBJohn restart
their reading of Genesis. This time the revision is more
detailed, with additional allusions as well as quotations
from Genesis 2–6 – though Genesis 1 is not ignored. “The
Human exists!” (14.1) is a declaratory interpretation of the
originally jussive statement, “Let light/man exist [γενηθήτω
φῶς/φώς]” (Gen 1.3). Genesis 1.26, in turn, is reread as a
quote from the rulers who make the animate Adam using
the model of the perfect Human (SBJohn 14.7).
   In this context, the plural “Let us” (Gen 1.26) makes
sense as a phrase spoken by many rulers. The creation of
Adam’s soul by lower powers (angels) is good Platonic
(and Philonic) tradition.10 What the rulers say, however,
changes the text of Genesis. It is not, “Let us make a human
according to our image and likeness,” but, “Let us build a
human according to the image of God and according to his
likeness” (14.7). The animate Adam is evidently not meant

10
     Plato, Timaeus 41a–42b; Philo, On the Creation of the World 73–75;
     On Flight and Finding 68–70; cf. Justin Martyr, Dialogue with
     Trypho 62.


                                   75
                    Part II The World of the Text


to resemble the planetary rulers, despite the fact that they
transmit their passions to his soul.11 In SBJohn III, Adam
only becomes like the rulers when put in a material body.
   Adam is supposedly named after Adamas (SBJohn
14.11), but the name ’adam (“humankind”) comes from
the Hebrew text of Genesis 1.26 and Adam as a personal
name from Genesis 2.16, 19. The rulers name their crea-
ture Adam so that his name and power might illumine
them. The assumption is that the model of Adam is a
light man, which again presumes the same double entendre
of φως in Genesis 1.3 (in the LXX).
   Yaldabaoth blowing divine breath into Adam rewrites
Genesis 2.7, where “Yahweh Elohim” in the Hebrew text
breathes into Adam’s nostril’s the breath of life. Only
by divine breath could Adam arise. “Breath” (πνοή) in
the Septuagint is taken to be equivalent to divine spirit
(πνεῦμα) – another Philonic reading.12 The mother’s light
power is also spirit (πνεῦμα).
   By receiving spirit, Adam’s animate form stirs with life;
but he is not just alive; Adam becomes “empowered” and
“wiser” than his builders. Adam’s wisdom was usually
read out of Genesis 2.19, where he named the animals (a
sign of intelligence). Adam is naked without shame (Gen
2.25) in the sense that he is “stripped,” not of clothes, but
“of evil” (SBJohn 15.11).13


11

                         4 Recreating Creation


   The creation of Adam’s material body (SBJohn 16.14–
18) was probably inspired from another clause of Gen
2.7, that Adam was made from dust or dirt (taken to be
base matter). Ancient interpreters like Philo assumed that
humanity had a double creation. First there was the cre-
ation of a non-fleshly Adam – what Philo called “a certain
form”14 – and then the creation of Adam’s fleshly body,
what Philo named the “dwelling of endless calamities.”15
   The description of Paradise as a place of delight full of
fruit-bearing trees (SBJohn 17.1) comes from Gen 2.8–9.
But the trees and delight are allegorized to refer to the
alluring entrapments of earthly sensations. The under-
standing of Eden as “delight” or “luxury” (τρυφή) comes
from Gen 3.23–24 (Septuagint). Philo had already made
the identification: “Eden means luxury (τρυφή).”16
   The Father’s sending Luminous Insight as a helper
(SBJohn 16.1–7) to Adam is an interpretation of Eve’s
creation (Gen 2.18). The fact that this Luminous Insight
is named “Life” (ζωή) is inspired by Gen 3.20. In this case,
Eve is spiritualized and distinguished from the material
woman later made out of Adam (SBJohn 18.6). When the
material woman is separated from Adam, Adam recog-
nizes Luminous Insight as his “joint essence” and calls
her ‘mother of the living’ (Gen 3.20; SBJohn 11.4–5). He
quotes Gen 2.23 (“This is bone from my bone …”) with
a spiritual meaning – that his true “essence” (Wisdom’s
light power) is the essence of his true spouse, Insight.

     Philo, On the Eternity of the World 134. Runia 2001, 323.
15
     Philo, On the Confusion of Tongues 177.
16
     Philo, Allegorical Interpretation 1.45. Cf. Questions and Answers on
     Genesis 1.7. “The name Eden, when translated, is certainly a symbol

                    Part II The World of the Text


   Adam and his wife eat of the tree of knowledge, as
they do in Gen 3.6. Their withdrawal from the cre-
ator (SBJohn 19.1) comes from Gen 3.8, where they
hide from him in the garden. Yaldabaoth’s curse stems
from Gen 3.16–19, including Adam’s rule over Eve.
The exile from Paradise is a literal reading of Gen
3.24. In SBJohn, the creator does not clothe Adam and
Eve with light (Gen 3.21).17 Rather, he clothes them
with darkness. The idea that Adam and Eve lost their
brilliance – a garment of light – after eating the for-
bidden fruit was traditional.18 In SBJohn, significantly,
the darkening happens before the eating. Adam did not
lose the light garment due to sin; rather, it was taken
from him.19
   The creator producing a child from Eve is an inter-
pretation of Eve’s words in Gen 4.1, “I obtained a man
with Yahweh.” In some Jewish interpretations, Satan (or
Samael) has intercourse with Eve.20 SBJohn could sim-
plify matters, since the creator Yaldabaoth and the devil
played the same role. “I obtained a man with Yahweh,”
meant that Eve had sex with the creator to produce Cain.
The idea that Cain was child of “the evil one” was tradi-
tional among both Jews and Christians.21

                                    78
                       4 Recreating Creation


   Yaldabaoth already had children to rule the sky (the
seven planetary authorities); he needed children to gov-
ern earth and the four elements. So he produced Cain
and Abel – or, according to their true names, “Yahweh
and Elohim.” The two names reflect the two names
for God in the Hebrew text of Genesis (often trans-
lated “Lord” and “God” respectively). Cain’s true name
“Yahweh” seems to be based on an alternative reading
of the Hebrew text of Gen 4.1, where the Hebrew ’et
is not read as the preposition “with,” but as a sign of
the direct object, thus, “I have acquired a man, [namely]
Yahweh.”22
   In Jewish tradition, Cain was said to have an angelic
appearance.23 In SBJohn, Yaldabaoth’s angels are ani-
malic, so it is no surprise that Yahweh and Elohim are
bestial. Elohim takes control of the upper elements, fire
and air, whereas Yahweh-Cain rules the lower elements,
earth and water. As lord of base matter, Yahweh-Cain is
also lord of death. All beings made of earth and water are
destined to die. Thus Cain fulfills his role as the first and
archetypal murderer.
   Adam fathers Seth, his true (spiritual) likeness, recall-
ing Gen 5.3. Seth becomes father of a favored family,
the likeness of the higher family among the eternities
(SBJohn 19.19). In Jewish tradition, Seth was said to
be honored and blessed.24 Josephus believed that Seth
excelled in virtue and left descendants who imitated his
virtue.25

   22
        Kugel 1998, 169.
   23
        Pirqei de Rabbi Eliezer 21; Targum Pseudo-Jonathan Gen 4.1.
   24
        Sir 49.16; Jubilees 19.24.
   25
        Josephus, Jewish Antiquities 1.68–69, 72.


                                  79
                    Part II The World of the Text


   Sethians took the step of making Seth, not Abraham,
father of the elect.
   In SBJohn, fate is the binding of star gods with “mea-
sures, times, and seasons” (21.5). The wording probably
alludes to Gen 1.14, where the stars are made “for signs
and seasons.”26
   The creator’s grief at the making of humanity (SBJohn
21.6) reflects Genesis 6.6: “God was grieved that he made
humanity upon the earth, and he reconsidered” (LXX). This
verse was (and remains) a thorn in the side of those arguing
for the creator’s faithful goodness. The author(s) of SBJohn,
however, could take the text literally. The creator regretted
the making of humankind – for humankind was the start of
his undoing. Humanity was never part of Yaldabaoth’s plan.
It was the plan of Providence to defeat him.
   In Genesis, the creator laments human wickedness; in
SBJohn, he rues the fact that humans perpetuate the light
power apart from his control. In both cases, the “solution”
is a great Flood from which Noah and select others are
saved. In SBJohn, Noah is not the creator’s prophet, but
an agent of Providence. Noah as the (unheard) preacher
of repentance was traditional lore by this time (1 Pet 3.20;
2 Pet 2.5).27
   Angels mixing with the daughters of men is a reading of
Gen 6.4. The details of this tradition derive not directly
from Genesis, however, but from the Watcher myth of
1 Enoch 1–36. SBJohn does not cite the Enochic Book of
the Watchers, but treats the story as if it were on par with
Genesis. At the same time, it changes the text of both

26
     Rightly seen by Spjut 2018, 95.
27
     Josephus, Jewish Antiquities 1.73; ApocPaul 50; Great Pow. 38.25–28.


                                    80
                          4 Recreating Creation


Genesis and 1 Enoch by placing the mating of Watchers
with women after the Flood. In this variant, human and
angel mating does not cause the vices which lead to the
Flood. Rather, it explains the predominance of human
ignorance after the Deluge.
   According to SBJohn, the women are initially immov-
able in the face of angelic seduction. (After all, they are the
“daughters of [true] human beings” [Gen 6.4] – the family
of Seth.) The angels resort to trickery with the promise of
material gifts, not astrological or metallurgical knowledge
(as in the Watcher myth). They also take the form of the
women’s husbands – a detail also found in the Testament
of Reuben 5.6–7. Yet this particular detail, however, taps
into more ancient mythemes, such as the story of Zeus
arriving as Alcmene’s husband to produce Heracles.
   John’s question about the counterfeit spirit’s origin
launches the retelling of the Watcher story. The coun-
terfeit spirit is not in the Book of Watchers, but could
be viewed as coming from the Two Spirits tradition. In
the Qumran Community Rule, we read that the “God
of knowledge” placed within humankind “two spirits,”
the spirit of truth and of deceit (cf. 1 John 4.6). These
two spirits produce generations of truth and deceit. Over
the generation of truth is the Prince of Lights (compare
Oroiael or the Light-Human Christ), and over the sons
of deceit is the Angel of Darkness (compare Yaldabaoth).
From the Angel of Darkness stems corruption and
vices, including lack of hearing and hardness of heart.28

28
     Community Rule 3.15–22; 4.11. In the Testament of Judah 20.1–3 the
     two spirits “wait upon man,” the spirit of truth and the spirit of deceit.
     In Barn 18.1, the ways of light and darkness are related to the light-
     bearing angels of God and angels of Satan.


                                      81
                    Part II The World of the Text


Structurally, such a backstory sounds like what we find in
SBJohn, apart from the fact that it is Yaldabaoth, not the
“God of knowledge,” who unleashes the counterfeit.
   Even someone as philosophical as Philo was aware of
two “powers” hidden in the human heart – one salutary,
the other destructive. These powers enter every soul
when it is born and inspire the woes of constant struggle.
God does not allow the destructive power to dominate
a sage’s soul. But the destructive spirit leads the wicked
into torment, harm, and contention.29
   In SBJohn, the counterfeit spirit had been introduced
before the Flood narrative. It appears as a component of
human flesh (SBJohn 16.19), as the tree of “life” which
causes Adam to regress (17.6), and as means by which the
rulers sow their (material) likeness (65.10).30
   The counterfeit spirit also clarifies the saying in Gen
6.3, that God’s (true) “spirit will not remain in humans
indefinitely, since they are flesh” (Septuagint). It preys
upon and pushes out the divine spirit given as a helper
to human beings (SBJohn 16.3; 19.22). It makes spiritual
humans carnal.
   The counterfeit spirit spreads widely because it is
injected into the human genome, as it were, presumably
through the semen of evil angels mating with women. The
offspring of these women are not rebellious giants – as in

     Philo, Questions and Answers on Exodus 1.23, commenting on

     strike.”

     On the counterfeit spirit, see further Böhlig 1968, 162–74; Losekam

     the two spirits theme back to two souls which rule the universe in
     Plato, Laws 896e–898b; cf. 904a–e.

                        4 Recreating Creation


the story of the Watchers – but those humans inflicted
with the trait of giants: stubbornness (SBJohn 21.26).
These pigheaded people are not automatically damned,
however. Rather, the mixing of angelic and human seed
explains why so many people require repentance and rein-
carnation to be redeemed.
   The ontological mismatch between humans and angels
is not highlighted as a problem in SBJohn. In accordance
with its ascetic interests, SBJohn III condemns delight in
material trinkets and lust. Whether or not sex is prohib-
ited outright, it is seen as a distraction from true wisdom
and the higher realm.31
   The revelation of Christ is the final counterpunch of
the forces of good to overcome evil. And the culmination
of Christ’s revelation is the text of SBJohn itself. In this
new scripture, Genesis is rewritten, to be sure, but is still
recognizable. The very fact that the authors tried to cor-
rect Genesis shows that they did not discard it.


                          Genesis Corrected
The author(s) of SBJohn directly correct Genesis (or
rather its presumed writer, Moses) on five occasions.
After the boast of Yaldabaoth, John asks about Wisdom’s
hovering. Jesus laughs and says:

You suppose it is as Moses said: “(she hovered) over the
waters.” (Gen 1.2). Not at all! Rather, she saw the wickedness
and rebellion which would erupt from her child. She repented

31
     On the Watcher story in SBJohn, see further Scopello 1980, 220–30;
     Brakke 2002, 41–62; Bull 2017, 75–107; Goff 2022, 238–61; Losekam
     2010, 151–83; Losekam 2010, 262–2, esp. 267–74.


                                   83
                    Part II The World of the Text

and wandered here and there in the darkness of ignorance. She
began to feel shame and did not dare to return. Rather, she
flitted back and forth. Her flitting back and forth is called “hov-
ering.” (SBJohn 13.5–10)

By nature, Wisdom “is more moving than any motion”
(Wisd 7.24). The immediate exegetical origin of Wisdom’s
movement, however, is Genesis 1.2 – she is the Spirit of
God who “hovers” over the face of the waters, here evi-
dently representing chaotic matter. Despite his rhetoric,
Christ does not reject the notion of Wisdom’s movement;
he allegorizes it. Wisdom’s wobble is the emotional flux
of her penitence. Like Adam and Eve in Eden (Gen 3.10),
Wisdom feels shame – not at her own deed specifically –
but at the vice and rebellion which explodes from her child.
   Later in the story, Jesus asserts that he encouraged
Adam to eat from the tree of knowledge. John asks,
“Wasn’t it the snake who actually instructed him?”
(17.16). Jesus again laughs and interprets the serpent
as the sowing (perhaps, ejaculation) of fleshly lust. This
interpretation of the serpent may be based on ancient
Jewish traditions of the serpent – a manifestation of the
devil – having intercourse with Eve.32 The serpent was
already a phallic symbol, and his sexual activity made him
a fitting symbol of sex.33
   John’s third interpretive question is about Adam’s
sleep. After his characteristic chuckle, Jesus replies,
You suppose it was as Moses said, that he “put him to sleep.”
Hardly! Rather, he smothered his perception with impercep-
tion. In fact, he said by the prophet, “I will make the ears of

  32
       4 Macc 18.7–8; 2 En 31.4–6; Pirqei de Rabbi Eliezer 21; 2 Cor 11.3.
  33
       Ref. 5.7.27–28 with Litwa 2022, 125–27.


                                     84
                        4 Recreating Creation

their heart sluggish, so that they neither understand nor see.”
(SBJohn 17.21–23)

Jesus clarifies that the creator does not put Adam to sleep
(Gen 2.21); rather Yaldabaoth afflicts Adam with a stu-
pefying trance. Jesus explains that this trance represents
cognitive dampening. This interpretation does not repre-
sent a complete rejection of Genesis or of scripture more
generally. In fact, Jesus corrects Moses by Isaiah; it is
Isaiah (6.10) who clarifies that the creator has the power
to cognitively incapacitate people.34
   Yaldabaoth planned to anaesthetize Adam in order
to remove Luminous Insight from inside him. But
Yaldabaoth was a blind surgeon, unable to follow Insight
with his scalpel.
   On this score, Jesus’ disagreement with Moses is rather
minor. Jesus does not deny that the creator constructed a
woman from Adam. He only denies that he used Adam’s
rib (Gen 2.22). His objection is not far different from
Philo’s: “How could anyone admit that a woman, or a
human being at all, came into existence out of a man’s
rib?”35 Presumably, however, it was still something from
Adam that allowed Yaldabaoth to build the earthly Eve.
   The final correction of Moses is that the seed of Seth
hid in a luminous cloud, not an ark of wood. But even
this correction depends on an informed reading of Jewish
scripture. The ark of Noah bleeds into the ark of the cov-
enant, which was occasionally said to be surrounded by
a luminous cloud.36 The fusing of Noah’s ark with the

            For the reception of Isa 6.10, see Litwa 2021a, 93–94.
       35
            Philo, Allegorical Interpretation 2.19.

ark of the covenant might seem a stretch to moderns,
but such catchword exegesis was customary in rabbinic
interpretations.
  In short, despite their combative stance toward Moses,
the author(s) of SBJohn closely adhered to Genesis and to
other biblical traditions.37


                       Beyond Genesis
The introduction of SBJohn is a continuation of GJohn,
and it portrays the character John, evidently the beloved
disciple, receiving special revelation from Jesus. The idea
that the apostles initially worshipped at the temple after
Christ’s ascension resembles some early Christian texts.
In GLuke 24.53, the apostles continue to worship in the
temple after Jesus’ ascension. In SBJohn, apostolic adher-
ence to the temple is seen as foolish. John only realizes
this after his polemical encounter with a Pharisee, which
mimics similar episodes in the Gospels. In the Gospels,
Jesus usually wins the argument with the scribes and
Pharisees. In SBJohn, John turns away from the temple
toward the true site of revelation: the mountain. Going
out to a deserted place to receive a revelation was tradi-
tional, and recalls Elijah and Jesus (among others) in the
wilderness.38 The mountain setting recalls the mount of
transfiguration (GMatt 17:1–8, par.)
   When Jesus is revealed, his opening description of the
Father seems, at first sight, solely philosophical. Yet it is
soaked with Christian language. The Father’s holiness

           37
                See further Creech 2017.
           38
                1 Kgs 19; GMatthew 4.1–11 and parallels.


                                 86
                         4 Recreating Creation


or sanctity is affirmed in the Hebrew Bible and in early
Christian literature,39 as is his invisibility.40 He rules as in
Psalm 97.1, “exists in a light” and is “Spirit” (SBJohn 4.5–
6), as in Johannine literature (1 John 1.5; GJohn 4.24).
The mercy of the Father mentioned later in the narrative
(SBJohn 16.1–3) is also indicative of the Christian God
(Eph 2.4).41
   By highlighting these attributes, SBJohn presents a
distinctly Christian theology. At the same time, almost
all Christian theology by the late second century had to
some degree already been informed by (a broadly Middle
Platonic) philosophy.
   The Father is “the fount of living waters” (SBJohn 5.1–
2) which bubble up into Barbelo. The well of inspiration
here was perhaps GJohn 7.38, “Rivers from his inward
parts will flow with living water,” and in particular 4.14,
where Jesus gives water which becomes a spring bubbling
with eternal life (compare Rev 21.6; 22.1, 17). Eternal
Life literally bubbles up from Barbelon after the Father’s
consent (SBJohn 6.9).
   Whatever her name means exactly, Barbelon functions
as the Wisdom personified in Jewish lore:

Before eternity, God founded me in eternity before he made
the earth and the abysses (Prov 8.23–24, LXX).

Before the eternity he founded me from the beginning (Sir
24.9).

For she is a mist of God’s power, an emanation of the Almighty’s
pure glory, and for this reason nothing defiled inflicts her, for
she is the beaming of his eternal light, a spotless mirror of
God’s activity; an image of his goodness (Wisd 7.25–26).

Barbelon is upper Wisdom, Eve undefiled, the per-
fect image of God. Lower Wisdom is the mother of
human souls. Philo called her “the firstborn mother of
all things.”42 But Christ, evidently in his capacity as the
Selfborn, calls her “our sister,” which has a precedent
in Proverbs 7.4 in the Septuagint: “Say that wisdom is
your sister.”
   The Son is called “Onlyborn” in SBJohn 7.4. In GJohn
1.18 Christ is the Onlyborn god. The Son’s main title
in SBJohn, however, is “Selfborn.” Although born from
Barbelon, the Selfborn is manifest from the Father. He
is like the Father’s light, but not equal to it (compare
Phil 2.6).
   The Selfborn is the “firstborn … of all those who
belong to the Father” (SBJohn 7.4). The title is remi-
niscent of “firstborn of all creation,” given to Christ in
Colossians 1.15. But the firstborn is not a creature, and
perhaps that is the very sense of “Selfborn.” All things
are made in him.43 He is the “beginning” spoken of in
Proverbs 8.22 and in Genesis 1.1.44
   The Father’s anointing shows that the Selfborn is not –
or not simply – Χρηστός (the Good one), but Χριστός (the
Anointed one). Christ is not anointed with physical oil,
but with goodness, as mediated by Barbelon. For Jesus

as the anointed, one can compare GLuke 4.16–19 and
Acts 10.38, combined with the baptismal traditions in
which Jesus is anointed by Spirit in the form of a dove
(GMatthew 3.13–17, par.). The Father completing Christ
uses language reminiscent of Hebrews 2.10 (where Jesus
is “made perfect” – but through suffering).
   The preexistent forms of saved human souls (the deni-
zens of the four realms) are chosen from the foundation of
the world to be sacred. These seeds of Seth are thus rem-
iniscent of the saints mentioned in Eph 1.4: “He chose
us in him to be holy before the foundation of the world.”
   When Yaldabaoth is produced in SBJohn III he appears
as a serpent and a lion. Yaldabaoth as lion suggests his
strength and audacity (“Your adversary the devil is like a
roaring, roaming lion, looking for someone to devour”).45
Yaldabaoth as snake indicates cleverness and craftiness
(“Be as wise as serpents,” GMatthew 10.16).
   Fire as Yaldabaoth’s characteristic element may indi-
cate raw sexual energy, rage, or jealousy, or a mix of
these. It is a distinctly biblical image: “Yahweh your god
is a consuming fire.”46
   Wisdom tries to “yoke” (perhaps marry) her son to a
cloud (in Greek mythology, Ixion has sex with a cloud
in the shape of Hera). Nevertheless, Yaldabaoth “yokes”
himself to Ignorance. Wisdom has her own throne in “a
pillar of cloud” (Sir 24.4), which in SBJohn she gives to
Yaldabaoth. According to Proverbs, the creator “separated
off his own throne upon the winds” (8.27). Yaldabaoth’s

throne in the middle of the cloud recalls the throne of

   Yaldabaoth’s boast can be broken up into two parts: A
claim to jealousy and a claim to exclusivity.48 The jealousy
claim comes out of the Ten Commandments (Exod 20.5;
Deut 5.6–8) as an explanation for why the creator does not
want Israelites to produce sacred images of other gods.
   The exclusivity claim is equally biblical, though it is
difficult to say from what text it derives exactly. There
are numerous passages in Isaiah, in Deuteronomy, and
elsewhere where the creator declares himself to be exclu-
sive.49 Formulas of exclusivity (“I and no other”) are
already treated as arrogant boasts in Isaiah 47.8–10.50
What seems like SBJohn’s innovation is already biblical.
   After Yaldabaoth’s boast, Wisdom weeps because her
“dark miscarriage” is incomplete (13.15). The weeping
is reminiscent of the groaning and sadness Yaldabaoth
inflicts on Eve (Gen 3.16). The image of miscarriage may
stem from Isaiah 14.19, where Lucifer is characterized
as a miscarriage in the Greek translation by Symmachus
and some Jewish targums. It was also Jewish tradition
that repentance was created before the universe.51 The

fact that Wisdom repented before the creation of Adam
(SBJohn 36.7) may echo this tradition.
   According to SBJohn, Adam is originally created as a
being of soul. The account may be based on 1 Cor 15.45:
“The first human Adam became a living soul.” Only Adam
endowed with the light power becomes spiritual and alive,
in the image of the heavenly Human (1 Cor 15.49).
   The idea that the creator could send a “lying” or “coun-
terfeit spirit” to deceive people may also be inspired by
1 Kgs 22.21–23, where Yahweh sends a lying spirit to
speak lies in the mouths of the prophets to deceive King
Ahab. A similar event occurs in 2 Thess 2.11, where
“God” (evidently the creator) sends a deluding influence
to deceive people in the end times.


                            Conclusion
SBJohn is awash with biblical intertexts and is only one or
two steps beyond a biblical pastiche.52 Treating Genesis
alone, one sees a chain of quotations, echoes, and allusions,
sometimes linked to the same verse (see Table 4.1).53
   The story of the upper world hits the high points of
Gen 1 along with a few references to Gen 3 and 5. In the
second retelling, there is sometimes a verse-by-verse inter-
action in Gen 2–3 (even if out of the biblical order). Only
the story of the lower world touches on Gen 4, 6, and 7.
   Admittedly, if one reads Genesis side by side with
SBJohn, the interpretations and narrative expansions
often seem a “bridge too far.” Knowledge of intervening

                               92
                       4 Recreating Creation


Jewish tradition (e.g., the Enochic lore of angels defil-
ing the daughters of men, the creator producing chil-
dren from Eve, and so on) is necessary to bridge the gap
between Genesis and SBJohn.
   SBJohn’s rich use of biblical tradition creates a sort of
paradox. It is immersed in Jewish traditions of exegesis
despite the text manifesting anti-Jewish features. The
combined result is a story that does not seem very Jewish
at all: The sovereign creator is transmuted into an evil and
ignorant demiurge.54 Abraham’s seed is not chosen for
redemption; rather, Seth is the ancestor of the immovable
family. The creator does not bring Wisdom into being;
instead, she births the creator.55 Other Mosaic traditions
are co-opted and corrected.
   Whereas most Jewish interpretations of Genesis (e.g.,
Philo’s On the Creation of the World) try to defend the
text by seeing in it an alternative meaning, SBJohn takes a
contrarian stance. Philo, for his part, had the challenging
task of reconciling the ineffable, merciful God and Father
with the all-too-human creator of Genesis. In a sense, the
author(s) of SBJohn had it easier. They could understand
the creator literally – as a jealous being who blows out air
and walks with apparent legs in Eden – while using alle-
gory to defame God, not defend him. (For instance, that
the Flood did not consist of water but of darkness.) In
most cases, Moses “corrected” by the author(s) of SBJohn
was Moses allegorized.
   There are only a few cases in which the author(s) of
SBJohn directly take Moses to task. In one case (Adam’s

       54
            Luttikhuizen 2006, 6; cf. Luttikhuizen 2004, 148–60.
       55
            Luttikhuizen 2006, 46.


                                   93
                    Part II The World of the Text


trance), Moses is corrected by another scriptural passage
(Isa 6.10). In another (Eve made from Adam’s rib), the cor-
rection seems almost like quibbling. Other corrections,
such as the ark as a cloud of light, are based on knowledge
of other biblical traditions. All in all, the hermeneutical
relation of SBJohn to Genesis is varied and ambivalent,
and it cannot be summed up in any single phrase, such as
a “transgressive interpretation,” or “revolt.”56
   The negative attitude of SBJohn’s author(s) – if sus-
tained against the creator – is not consistently maintained
against the text of Genesis 1–7 or any other scripture.57
SBJohn’s author(s), like virtually all biblical interpreters,
were pickers and choosers. However much they rejected
certain elements of Genesis as wrong or naïve, they con-
stantly used Genesis and other biblical texts as a spring-
board, the foundation for conceiving a new and truer
revelation.58
   The author(s) of SBJohn followed the structure of
Genesis, included many of its characters, and pursued its
themes – but they also transformed the text into some-
thing new. First, they recontextualized the story as a
revelation of Christ to John. In so doing, they shifted
its genre from creation myth to revelation dialogue.
They expanded its timeline as far back as eternity past
and as far forward as Christ, filling in many perceived
gaps (and creating several others). They introduced new

56
     For transgressive interpretation, see DeConick 2013a, 156–59;
     DeConick 2013b, 300–01; for “revolt” as a hermeneutical principle,
     see Pearson 1990, 37.
57
     Contrary to Busch 2017, 245.
58
     Pearson 1988, 635–52; Williams 1996, 54–79; Painchaud 1996,
     129–46; Dunderberg 2011; Busch 2018, 195–217.


                                    94
                        4 Recreating Creation


characters (for instance, Barbelon, the Selfborn, and the
four Lights). They morally transformed traditional char-
acters, demeaning some (the creator and the serpent),
while exalting others (Seth, Noah, Seth’s seed).
   The author(s) of SBJohn probably understood Genesis
as authoritative insofar as it was considered to be the report
of the creator speaking through Moses. (The creator was
not dead wrong, but half correct.) At the same time, the
claim that Moses was wrong at several points cannot be
reduced to mere rhetoric.59 It indicates that Genesis did
not – or no longer – served as an ultimate, divine author-
ity. Moses could at times be right but, generally speaking,
Genesis was considered a book of half-truths and naïve
interpretations that cried out for completion and correc-
tion.60 Once the corrections were made, Genesis could
reasonably be replaced by SBJohn.61
   At the same time, it is clear that without Genesis
there is no SBJohn. From the perspective of SBJohn’s
author(s), they “redeemed” the text of Genesis by breath-
ing into it the living and inspired breath of Christ. These
writers evidently considered themselves to be part of a
better covenant than the one provided by Moses, and to
belong to a better family than Abraham’s. Armed with
greater knowledge, they took it upon themselves to write
the “true” story of the creation. The result was a new,
more philosophical and more plausible Genesis meant for
Christian gnostics.

59                                   60
     Contrary to Creech 2017, 118.        King 2006, 180–81.
61
     Contrary to Gilhus 1993, 54.




                                     95
                             5
                Myth as Philosophy
                             u


SBJohn cannot be explained without Genesis; but neither
can it be understood without philosophy. So much of its
content has been enriched by the subtle synthesis of phil-
osophical lore – whether it be Platonist, Neopythagorean,
Stoic, or Aristotelian in origin. This chapter surveys dis-
tinct philosophical themes that inform SBJohn, namely,
negative theology, lower demiurgy, number theory, con-
nections with Plato’s Timaeus, the notion of fate, and
evil as involuntary. The aim is not to introduce or to fully
explain all these underlying philosophical tenets – there are
handbooks for that – but to illumine the content of SBJohn.


                         The One
The authors of SBJohn saw philosophical notions glint-
ing like gold flecks in the fabric of Genesis. Originally,
Genesis was a Hebrew myth composed by elite scribes
in a small temple state in the southern Levant. These
scribes did not have a philosophical concept of God.
The being called “God” and “Lord God” in Genesis is
a skilled builder, to be sure, but he belongs to this uni-
verse, dwelling above the dome of the firmament. He
strolls through the garden of Eden, asks questions about
Adam’s whereabouts, jealously exiles the first couple
from Eden, and sets a guard over the tree of life (Genesis
3). Depending on which creation story one reads, the god

                             96
                      5 Myth as Philosophy


of Genesis seems sovereign or petty; he is no infinite,
unknowable Father.
   Such a Father – beyond knowledge and being itself –
needed to be inserted from Platonism. In the Parmenides,
we learn that a being called “One” is neither whole nor
partitioned (137c–d); it has no beginning, middle, or
end; it is unlimited, without shape (137e); is nowhere
and uncontained (138a–b); it does not move spatially or
by inward change (138c–e). It is neither like nor unlike
anything (139e–140a); it is neither equal nor unequal to
itself or to another (140b), not older or younger than
itself (140e), not in time (141a), or even in existence
(141e); it has no name, and is beyond knowledge and
perception (142a).1
   Some of these (non)descriptors make it into SBJohn. For
instance, SBJohn calls the Invisible Spirit “one,” without
limits, without parts, without name, without lack, and with-
out temporal constraints. Other Platonic traits, however,
do not fit SBJohn’s Father. After all, the Father emanates
Barbelon by a process of self-contemplation, and Barbelon
gives rise to the Selfborn. Both beings are modeled on the
One, so the One must be like them in some respect.


                       Deferred Demiurgy
The idea of a fully transcendent God who has no deal-
ings with creation and does not rule over the lower
world reflects the teaching of Plato’s student Aristotle.
According to Aristotle, the highest God (conceived of as a
divine Mind or Nous) is not engaged in practical activity;

             1
                 See further Turner and Corrigan 2010.


                                 97
                    Part II The World of the Text


he is not a world maker. The providence of this God does
not extend below the moon.2 Consequently, the cosmic
rulers of our world had to be conceived as junior officers
like star gods and daimones.3 Second-century Platonists
like Numenius taught that the chief creator is different
from the supreme God.4
   When Platonists assimilated such doctrines, the condi-
tions were ripe for the author(s) of SBJohn to distinguish
a pure and good God – a supermind – from a skilled but
finite creator of this world. For his part, Plato outsourced
the creation of humanity to “younger” gods to prevent
the Demiurge from creating anything with the potential
to become evil.5 Genesis 1 says next to nothing about the
mechanics of humanity’s creation, let alone its creators. It
mentions only a plural, “Let us make,” in verse 26 – with
the “us” left dangling and undefined.
   SBJohn clarifies humanity’s creation. It makes the
planetary rulers the direct creators of humanity (inciden-
tally explaining why astrology works, because the plan-
etary rulers share their energies with the human soul.)
Plato viewed the traditional gods, led by Zeus, as human-
ity’s creators. But several of these Olympians shared
their names with the planets (Zeus is Jupiter, Aphrodite
is Venus, Hermes is Mercury, and so on). It is possible,
then, to retrace how the author(s) of SBJohn – among
other Platonists – connected the dots. The creation of
humanity by lower gods is also a creation by the planets,

    Ps.-Aristotle, On the Cosmos 397b9–400b5 in Thom 2015, 43–53.
4
    E.g., Numenius, frag. 12; cf. Plutarch, On the E at Delphi 21.393f–94a.
    See further O’Brien 2015; Luttikhuizen 2017, 277–88.
5
    Plato, Timaeus 41c–d.


                                    98
                        5 Myth as Philosophy


or the powers they represent (cf. the Hermetic Kore
Kosmou in Stobaean Hermetica 23.28–29).
   Of course, both Platonists and Aristotelians denied that
the creator(s) of the world and humankind were evil. One
of the most basic assumptions of Plato’s Timaeus is that the
creator – sometimes called demiurge, or craftsman – is fun-
damentally good and lavishes this goodness upon the world.6
   When philosophers distinguished between creator and
(high) God, however, it became possible to reevaluate
the creator’s character. Platonizing Christians could read
Genesis without the assumption that the creator and the
Father were the same. Like Marcion, they could then use
Genesis to prove that the creator was not only different
from God, but also malign. Christians did not derive the
evil creator from Platonism; but, thanks to Platonism,
they could find him in Genesis.


                           Number Theory
In the early Roman Empire, Neopythagoreans formed a
wing of the Platonic school and cultivated distinctive phil-
osophical emphases. One of them was that numbers serve
as the first principles of the cosmos. The Monad, repre-
sented by a geometrical point, was thought to become (or
alternatively, emanate) the Dyad, indicating a line (the
meeting of two points), then a Tetrad (a plane figure).
In turn, the Tetrad adds up to a Decad (1+2+3+4=10),
a three-dimensional solid. These solids, in different
arrangements, make up the four elements.7

 6
     Plato, Timaeus 29d–e. Cf. also Numenius, frag. 16, 20 (Des Places).
 7
     Diogenes Laertius, Lives of Philosophers 8.25.


                                   99
                    Part II The World of the Text


  In good Neopythagorean fashion, the author(s) of
SBJohn used numbers to speak of the eternities in the
upper world. The Father, for instance, is directly called
“Monad” (SBJohn 4.2). Nicomachus of Gerasa (about
60–120 ce) reportedly said that God “coincides with the
Monad, since he is seminally everything which exists.”8
Other Neopythagoreans called the Monad “pure light,
most authoritative over everything in general … sun-like
and ruling.”9 They thought of the Monad as an intellect,
and named it “being, cause of truth, simple, paradigm,
order, concord,” among other things.10
  In some way, the Dyad emerged from the Monad.
Neopythagoreans called her “daring” and “impulse,”11
as well as movement, generation, and change.12 She is
the cause of alteration, and is associated with deficiency,
excess, matter, and formlessness.13 This description of the
Dyad does not fit the stable character of Barbelon, but it is
reminiscent of her distant daughter Wisdom, who shud-
ders with powerful feelings of shame and repentance. In
other Christian theologies (such as Simonianism), Dyad-
Thought falls into this world. The author of SBJohn was
not happy to have his second principle suffer lack, so the
deficiency of the Dyad was deferred to a lower image of
divine intellect, Wisdom.

 8
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 1.3 (trans. Waterfield 1988, 37).
 9
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 1.3 (trans. Waterfield 1988, 37).
10
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 1.6 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 37).
11
     Plutarch, Isis and Osiris 75 (Mor. 381f).
12
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 1.7–8 (trans. Waterfield 1988,
     41); Burkert 1972, 21–22.
13
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 11–12 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 44–45).


                                     100
                         5 Myth as Philosophy


   The Neopythagorean Triad emerges when Monad and
Dyad interact (1+2=3). According to Neopythagoreans,
the number three is the active form of the Monad.14 It is
the form of the completion of all things, giving all things
equality and fulfillment.15 If the Selfborn was conceived
as the Triad, it is logical that he represents the Monad –
like a spark signals light – and is God over all.
   Another number highlighted in SBJohn is the Pentad,
a number associated with wholeness and justice.16 It sym-
bolizes generation and “marriage” since it marries the
odd (three) and even (two).17 “It produces either itself or
ten, that is to say, either its own characteristic or the per-
fect whole.”18 In SBJohn, the Pentad, since it contains
male and female characters, gives rise to the Decad.
   Neopythagoreans called the Decad “eternity,” since
eternity is complete and everlasting and brings all things
to fulfillment. The Decad is what creates the finished
products and the universe, setting a limit to infinitude.19
In sum, “the whole universe is manifestly completed
and enclosed by the Decad, and seeded by the Monad;
it gains movement thanks to the Dyad and life thanks to
the Pentad.”20

     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 14 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 49).
15
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 17–18 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 51).
16
     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 37 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 70).

     Plutarch, On the E at Delphi 7, 388a-b.

     Plutarch, On the E at Delphi 7, 388e

     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 83 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 112).

     Ps.-Iamblichus, Theology of Arithmetic 32 (trans. Waterfield
     1988, 66).


                                   101
                   Part II The World of the Text


   To be sure, this Neopythagorean philosophy of num-
bers cannot be neatly mapped onto SBJohn. Twelve, for
instance, was never valorized by Neopythagoreans, but
it was important to the author(s) of SBJohn. One sus-
pects this was because of Jewish and Christian influence:
Twelve tribes and twelve apostles – among other biblical
“twelves” – made the number sacred.21 The twelve signs
of the zodiac were also in the background. Accordingly,
Christian theologians consistently made the Dodecad,
not the Decad, the outer limit of the Fullness.
   Neopythagoreans had much to say about the number
seven. In SBJohn, seven appears to be of no importance
in the upper world; the seventh day (or Sabbath) is the
birthday of the lower world only. Moreover, SBJohn does
not appear very interested in the Ogdoad (eight). We do
not see the 8–10–12 pattern of aeonic proliferation forged
by Valentinian theologians, for instance.22 The author(s)
of SBJohn may have been in dialogue with Valentinians,
but went their own way.


                   Plato’s Story of Creation
Plato’s Timaeus tells of a creator who appears on the
cosmic stage with two other characters: A receptacle (a
place or medium in which the four elements can meld
and take shape) and a “perfect Animal” (meaning the
combined world of Forms). The receptacle houses the
world of becoming (material phenomena in flux), whereas
the perfect Animal exemplifies true being (stable mental

  21
       Irenaeus, AH 1.3.2; 1.18.4. See further Kalvesmaki 2013, 46–47.
  22
       Kalvesmaki 2013, 27–83.


                                  102
                           5 Myth as Philosophy


realities). The goal of the creator is simple: To make the
world of flux as much like the world of Forms as he can.
He does so by adding harmonies and proportions to a
wasteland of erring elements.
   Yet Plato’s creator has limits. He cannot – and does
not wish to – violate the laws of physics by making some-
thing out of nothing. He is constrained by Necessity –
aka “the errant cause” – by which was apparently meant
the actual structures and possibilities inherent in mat-
ter.23 Accordingly, the world necessarily fell short of
the truth and beauty exemplified by the Forms. Matter
can never be tamed to become an exact representation
of spirit.
   At the same time, Plato’s creator – working with
assistants – does the best he can. He begins by design-
ing the World Soul, the universe’s invisible brain,
according to whose motions stars and planets revolve
in their orbits. After the planets, the creator makes the
pantheon of deathless gods (Zeus, Hermes, Athena, and
the rest). These gods in turn made organisms in their
own shape – rational and beautiful, to be sure, but des-
tined to decay.
   These “young gods” make the irrational souls of
men – by which is meant male humans – whose spher-
ical heads contain the same proportioned motions that
govern the stars.24 Plato believed that males were made
first, and from reincarnated men came women, then
four-footed beasts, flying creatures, swimmers, and
swarmers (not necessarily in this order). A deeply patri-
archal theory, Plato’s grand vision turns Darwinian

23                          24
     Plato, Timaeus 48a.         Plato, Timaeus 43e; 69c.


                                      103
                   Part II The World of the Text


evolution on its head. According to Plato, the first crea-
tures were male humans, and all other earthly animals
(from lions to bugs) are progressively devolved versions
of this perfect specimen.
   The creation stories in both the Timaeus and Genesis
are myths (traditional stories important to local commu-
nities, but not empirically verified or universally true).
Whatever Genesis lacked in philosophical respectability
and the scientific register of its language, the author of
SBJohn made up for by generating a philosophical dia-
logue. He heated Genesis in the forge of his Platonizing
imagination and pounded it with his hammer to make
it seem more likely. The recreated story of creation
thus answered a new generation’s scientific and moral
questions.


                         The Four Elements
We can examine several aspects of SBJohn inspired, ulti-
mately, by the Timaeus. Genesis says nothing about the
four elements (earth, air, fire, and water), but Plato does.
“Nothing could ever become visible,” he writes, “apart
from fire, nor tangible without something solid, nor solid
without earth.”25 To combine fire and earth, the creator
“set water and air” between them, “and made them as
proportionate to one another as possible, so that what fire
is to air, air is to water, and what air is to water, water is to
earth. He then bound them together and thus constructed
the visible and tangible universe.”26 Plato later described


25                             26
     Plato, Timaeus 31c–32c.        Plato, Timaeus 53c–56c.


                                    104
                        5 Myth as Philosophy


the “molecular” structures of the four elements.27 The
author(s) of SBJohn were not interested in the shapes of
the four elements or their order. They were concerned
with defining the lords of these elements – Yahweh and
Elohim (SBJohn 19.12–13). Yet the very fact that they
assumed an elemental system is part of their Platonic
revision.


                          Planetary Powers
Genesis names only two great lights as mistresses of day
and night (1.14–18). Plato describes seven wandering
stars and an upper starry circle.28 Seven of these revolu-
tions were planetary, and an eighth circled above it, ever
fixed in its speed and the direction of its orbit.
   The author(s) of SBJohn devoted considerable time to
the seven planetary rulers, whom they considered to be
the chief operating officers of the universe. They believed
in an eighth realm of fire ruled by Yaldabaoth. Indeed,
Yaldabaoth’s “eternal realm of flame, of fire, and of light
in which he now resides” (11.10) is reminiscent of Plato’s
heavenly “province of fire, where the greatest mass of fire
is gathered.”29


                           Correspondence
As above, so below. The master idea that the lower world
corresponds to the higher is Platonic to the core.30 Plato’s
creator deliberately tries to make the fluctuating world of

27
     Plato, Timaeus 53c–56c. 28 Plato, Timaeus 38c, 39b.
29
     Plato, Timaeus 63b. 30 Plato, Timaeus 39e.


                                 105
                   Part II The World of the Text


matter as close to the model as he can. SBJohn asserts
the same kind of correspondence, but makes it incidental
and involuntary. That is to say, Yaldabaoth knew some of
the forms and patterns of the upper world, not because
he had seen it, but because he had taken his Mother’s
light power (SBJohn 11.7). With a shadow of a memory
he mistook for native genius, Yaldabaoth conformed cre-
ation to the hierarchies and numerical structures of the
higher world.


                         The True Human
According to Plato, what constitutes true humanity is not
flesh or feeling, but intellect (aka spirit). In the Timaeus,
the creator sends down the minds he made equal in num-
ber to the stars.31 The gods then weave these deathless
intellects to the structures of the lower soul (affects and
appetites) as well as to matter. Humanity is tripartite:
Flesh, soul, and the higher mind. (And for the Platonist,
mind is as superior to the soul as the soul is to the body.32)
   In SBJohn, rulers create the human soul and flesh, and
the enlightened mind (or spirit) consists of the Mother’s
light power. This spirit is the “daimon” who serves as the
second or higher self of human beings.33 The details of
these systems are obviously different, but the dualism is
comparable: The essential human is a deathless refugee
from the upper world while the flesh and the seat of the
emotions are secondary and not truly human.

31
     Alcinous, Handbook of Platonism 16.2; 23.1; Maximus of Tyre,
     Orations 33.7 (humans have a body from mortal discord, and
     intelligence from an outflow of the divine).
32
     Plutarch, On the Face in the Moon 943a. 33 Plato, Timaeus 90a.


                                  106
                          5 Myth as Philosophy


    Aristotle in particular fostered the idea of a divine
 element, received from the transcendent God, devel-
 oping in humanity. The Stagirite was the first to con-
 sistently emphasize that the human soul (ψυχή) and
­intellect (νοῦς) were different. The nous comes in from
 the outside.34 It does not need a material substrate. It is,
 rather, likened to a light which illumines the intelligi-
 bles.35 According to Aristotle, humanity’s nous-potential
 is actualized through contact with the divine Mind.36
 Such a notion comes to fruition in SBJohn. The light
 power is present in all people as a potential, which only
 the redeemed activate.37


                             Transmigration
Toward the end of SBJohn, John asks Jesus about souls
which do not know “the All.” Jesus explains that these
souls are not wicked in themselves. The counterfeit spirit
weighs heavy upon them. They are dragged into vice and
forced to forget their nature. After they die, the rulers
shunt them into new material sleeves. They live life again
until freed from fiery emotions and forgetfulness (SBJohn
20.15–30).
   Transmigration does not jibe with Judaism, but perco-
lates through Platonism. According to Plato, if a man fails
to master his emotions and to live justly during life, he is
“born a second time, now as a woman.” And if he still does

34
     Aristotle, Generation of Animals 2.3, 736b27–29.
35
     Aristotle, On the Soul 3.5 (430a 16–18). For this interpretation, see
     Luttikhuizen, 2006, 39–40.
36
     Aristotle, Metaphysics 12.7 (1072b20–30).

     Luttikhuizen 2006, 41.

not refrain from vice, he is “changed once again, this time
into some wild animal that resembled the wicked character
he had acquired. And he would have no rest from these
toilsome transformations until he had dragged that massive
accretion of fire-water-air-earth [the material body] into
conformity with” the rational motion of the fixed stars.38
   Transmigration allows for biological devolution, but
the point of it is to improve one’s nature – quite literally
by becoming male. For Plato, the end goal (for philo-
sophical males) was to return to the primeval dwelling
in one’s “companion star.”39 For SBJohn – which never
speaks of fleeing femininity – one returns to the divine
Fullness. This ascent involves residence in one of the
four “Lights,” which in Hebrew vernacular could refer
to stars.40

                         Father, Mother, Son
There are subtler connections between SBJohn and
Platonism. The primal divine triad in SBJohn consists of
Father, Mother, and Child. There is nothing quite like
this notion in Jewish scripture. To be sure, God is some-
times depicted as Father (e.g., Deut 1.31); Wisdom can
be seen as mother or creatrix (Prov 8.22–31); and some-
one like a son of man debuts briefly in Dan 7.13–14. But
it takes a vibrant imagination to combine these passages
and make a story of the divine first family.
   In Plato, we have a compact remark outlining the three
constituents of the universe: “That which comes to be, that
in which it comes to be, and that according to which what

38                                        39
     Plato, Timaeus 42b–c; cf. 89e–92c.        Plato, Timaeus 42b.
40
     King 2013, 9.


                                   108
                          5 Myth as Philosophy


comes to be is modeled.” Plato likens “what receives” to
“a mother, the source to a father, and the nature between
them to their child.”41 In this model, the father is not the
creator, but the world of forms; the mother is the recep-
tacle; and the child is the world. In SBJohn, of course, the
Father is Spirit, the Mother is Barbelon, and the Child is
the Selfborn. The referents are different, but the triadic
and familial structure overlaps.42


                            Wisdom’s Tremors
John asks Jesus why Mother Wisdom “hovers” after
Yaldabaoth boasts. Jesus explains that Wisdom does not
literally flit over waters. Instead, she moves back and
forth as a sign of her changed heart (SBJohn 13.1–10).
The obvious source for this image is Genesis 1.2, that
“the Spirit (or breath) of God hovered over the waters.”
Fascinatingly, a similar image appears in the Timaeus.
The receptacle, when it acquires the properties of the
four elements (heat, dryness, wetness, cold) assumes their
unbalanced qualities. Consequently, it “sways irregularly
in every direction as it is shaken.”43
   It is easy to imagine how someone familiar with both
Genesis and Timaeus could associate these passages. The
receptacle – interpreted as Wisdom – shakes due to her
interaction with matter. Wisdom, in SBJohn, shudders

in the face of Yaldabaoth’s wickedness and rebellion

Wisdom’s wavering.
   This discussion raises the question of how else Wisdom
resembles the receptacle. In SBJohn, Wisdom is not the space
of the material world, but she is still, as it were, the wetnurse
of becoming, for it is her act of singular self-​representation
that catalyzes the creation through her child.



                        The Chain and the Cave
The author(s) of SBJohn were not limited to Plato’s
Timaeus. They could have used other dialogues or –
more likely – Platonic handbooks. A widespread Platonic
idea is that the material body is a kind of “chain” (SBJohn
16.20). One will not find anything like this image in
Genesis, which describes how the creator molded a man
from mud.44
   The body as cave metaphor, moreover, can be traced to
Plato’s Republic, book 7. Here Plato likens human per-
ception to the perception of men chained to the wall of
a cave, seeing the shadows of puppets flashed onto a sili-
con screen. In SBJohn III, the “cave” is identified directly
with “the formation of the body” (16.20), ruled by the
lords of matter (Cain and Abel = Yahweh and Elohim).
The mother’s spirit has the power to bring humans out of
amnesia and from the “evil of the cave” (19.21).

44
     Gen 2.7. Plato, Phaedo 67d; cf. Philo, That the Worse Attacks
     the Better 159; Diodorus, Historical Library 4.42; Clement, The
     Instructor 8.65.2; Miscellanies 4.3.12.6; Epictetus, Discourses 1.9.11.

                          5 Myth as Philosophy


                            Sex as Secondary
In SBJohn, the secondary nature of sex and sexual desire
may also be built on a Platonic foundation. According
to Plato, men were not originally made into sexual
beings, for humanity was originally an all-male frater-
nity.45 When females emerged, the gods invested males
with animal desire. Seed is produced in brain marrow
and this marrow was instilled with a life-giving desire
for emission at the “place of venting.” “This is why, of
course, the male genitals are unruly and self-willed, like
an animal that will not be subject to reason, driven crazy
by its desires.”46
   Plato’s evaluation of sexual desire (unruly and over-
powering) is not altogether cheery, but the picture
becomes outright sinister in SBJohn. Yaldabaoth rapes
Eve (SBJohn 19.8–10). At this point, Yaldabaoth tries to
make Adam like himself by sowing in him the lust for
reproducing. His plan is to allow his minions to have sex
with more humans to produce children through them by
their counterfeit spirit (a “spirit” evidently transmitted
through semen, 19.16). “Till this day,” we learn, “sex was
instituted and perpetuated” by Yaldabaoth (19.15). It is
challenging to find anything positive about sex in SBJohn
III, except that some good offspring (e.g., Seth and his
descendants) are its presumed result. To be sure, a neg-
ative attitude toward sex need not derive entirely from
Platonism, but – once again – Platonism set the condi-
tions for a darker development.47

     Plato, Timaeus 91a. 46 Plato, Timaeus 91b.
47
     Philo identified sexual desire as the source (or beginning) of wrongs
     and crimes (On the Creation of the World 152).
