                                   Fate
According to SBJohn, Yaldabaoth fathered fate. A vari-
ety of Greco–Roman philosophers spoke of fate, but
Stoics were the most famous for the idea that “every-
thing that happens does so by fate.”48 They spoke of
fate as a “sequence” or “interweaving of causes,”49 “a
certain everlasting ordering of the whole,”50 whose
effects could not be avoided.51 Fate governs a cycle of
events that repeats because the same causes for events
continually circle throughout time.52 Stoics under-
stood fate as the rational principle (logos) of things gov-
erned by providence.53 They often identified fate and
providence.54
   For their part, Platonists tended to distinguish prov-
idence from fate, seeing the latter as a subsidiary prov-
idence.55 In this way, they could ensure that God was
blameless for the moral and physical wrongs rampant on
earth. Higher providence denotes the government of the
divine world, while fate is the government of the material
world. Lower providence made use of subordinate agents
(daimones – some of whom were not fully good or free
from fretting about material matters). God is by defini-
tion above fate. The same is true for human spirits, but

     Ps.-Plutarch, On Fate 568d; cf. Cicero, On Fate 30; Origen, Against
     Celsus 2.20.

     Ps.-Plutarch, Moralia 569c.

     Stobaeus, Eclogues 1.79.1 (quoting Chrysippus).

     Tertullian, 1 Apology 21.10; Aetius, Tenets 1.27.5. For more detailed
     accounts of Stoic fate, see; Brennan 2005, 235–41; Burns 2020, 37–44.
55
     E.g., Apuleius, Plato and His Doctrine 1.12.

                        5 Myth as Philosophy


their bodies are in the realm of fate (the material world).
Even though humans perform actions in fate’s realm, not
everything they do is fated.56
   In astrological and Hermetic lore, fate is associated
with the stars. It “is the cause of astral formations”57
and it rules the seven spheres.58 Philo assumed that,
for astrologers, “the circuits of sun and moon and of
the other heavenly bodies determine for every being in
existence both good things and their opposites.”59 He
opposed people (apparently fellow Jews) who believed
that Moses “represents fate and necessity as causes of
all events.”60 Moses admitted, according to Philo, that
the constellations and astral revolutions are causes, but
they are not primary causes.61 There is only one primary
cause – the creator. When speaking of the lower world,
the author of SBJohn would probably agree: The cre-
ator fathered fate.
   But fate, in SBJohn III, is no gift. It is a “chain” (21.5)
activated when not just people, but planets, daimones, and
angels are fixed in “measures, times, and seasons” (21.5).
Fate is never directly said to work through the stars. It
seems, rather, to be the experience of being caught in the
wheel of time (though stars mark the times and seasons).
In its own way, then, SBJohn assumes the Platonic distinc-
tion between providence and fate. Providence (Barbelo’s

     Ps.-Plutarch, Moralia 574d (LCL 405.355); cf. Alcinous, Handbook of

     431–47.

     Philo, Who is the Heir? 301.

                                   113
                    Part II The World of the Text


very name) is the ordered government of the upper world;
fate is the regime of Yaldabaoth.62
   Higher providence is focused on human beings, while
fate focuses on the cosmos. Higher providence (essen-
tially, the activity of Wisdom) is “anthropocentric:” It
works to ensure the salvation of true humanity, or the
light power.63 Yaldabaoth governs the world, and his
government (or fate) is openly hostile to true humanity.
This position is admittedly different from the views of
providence put forward by Christians like Irenaeus. For
Irenaeus, a single God cares for humanity and the lower
world. But the theory of SBJohn is not unchristian because
it disagrees with Irenaeus. There are two worlds to take
care of: Upper and lower. The true God, the god over
all – identified with Christ – cares for the upper world.
In the end, this is the only world that matters, since the
world of matter is destined for destruction.
   According to SBJohn III, fate is associated with – but
not identical to – sexual desire,64 the counterfeit spirit,65
and astral influences. Sexual desire is implanted in Adam
before fate is discussed. The same point holds for the
counterfeit spirit, which is an alien impulse integrated
into humans when they are given fleshly bodies. Fate
is also distinct from the notion that humanity’s lower
soul (comprising drives and desires) is made up of plan-
etary energies. If, however, one understands fate as
Yaldabaoth’s regime, which includes astral influences, the

     Burns 2020, 165. For the doctrine of three providences in Platonism,

counterfeit spirit, and sexual desire, then one can associ-
ate these countermeasures with fate.
   Neither philosophers nor the author(s) of SBJohn
feared fate. After all, by its very nature, fate only applies
to material bodies in the cosmos made by the archons.66
The human intellect (the light power of Wisdom) is natu-
rally above fate, Yaldabaoth’s tyrannical order of time and
material causation.67 The author(s) of SBJohn did not fret
if their bodies were limited by time and caught in a chain
of cause and effect. Ultimately, they would abandon the
flesh to fate, because the “chain” and “cave” was never the
self in the first place.68


                        Evil Is Involuntary
Platonists and SBJohn agree that evil is involuntary. No
rational human, with unclouded mind, would willingly
choose evil.69 False sense perceptions and misjudgments fog
and damage the mind so that it fails to make the best deci-
sions. The mind is misled by pleasure (the “supreme lure”70),
and reason is dominated by violent emotions like rage and
lust.71 Being dominated by these drives is not sin but illness.
The greatest sickness is ignorance, mother of misjudgments.

66
     Burns 2020, 317, “Gnostic myths such as the Apocryphon of John …
     suggest that our material and animate components … – the seats
     of our corporeal and sensate selves – are controlled by malevolent,
     external beings.”
67
     Cf. Corpus Hermeticum 4.1–2; Hermetic Fragments 20–21a; 39a;
     Clement, Excerpts from Theodotus 78.1.
68

69
     Plato, Timaeus 86b–d; Gorgias 467c–68e; Republic 4.438a, 6.505d; cf.
     Alcinous, Handbook of Platonism 29, 31.
70
     Plato, Timaeus 69d. 71 Apuleius, Plato and His Doctrine 2.4


                                   115
                    Part II The World of the Text


   The goal of life is to extirpate the passions – wild
and negative emotions like rage, jealousy, lust, and
greed (SBJohn 20.8). Cultivating the mind (or the light
power) is the main human virtue. Most everything else is
indifferent. The immovable family is just that – immov-
able – not disturbed or “caught” by material traumas or
tragedies, using the flesh as a mere tool (20.9). Such is the
life of the sage.72
   But if nondisturbance cannot be attained, it is suffi-
cient to change one’s mind or disposition and flee the
control of vice (SBJohn 20.15).73 We might liken this
ethical position to Aristotelian metriopatheia – the control
and balancing of the passions. In reality, most readers of
SBJohn were not free from disturbance or desire; they
were climbing the steep road of virtue. This is a social
reality that Stoics well understood.74 In theory, there
were sages who could completely extirpate the passions.
But they were as rare as the phoenix.75


                             Conclusion
SBJohn is a Christian master story that is most obviously
based on the book of Genesis. At the same time, it is shot
through with philosophical ideas and assumptions. Most
of these ideas are (Middle) Platonic in origin, though a
sprinkling of Neopythagorean, Stoic, and Aristotelian
ideas leaven the work throughout. Of course, SBJohn
represents a distinctive combination and application of
these philosophical ideas. Many ancient Platonists – not to

72
     Seneca, Epistles 120.10–14. See further King 1995, 88.
73
     On tiers of salvation, see Kocar 2021, 72–73, 82–91.
74
     Seneca, Epistles 75.8–10, 13–16. 75 Seneca, Epistles 42.1.


                                   116
                  5 Myth as Philosophy


mention Christians like Irenaeus – would have been (and
were) shocked by the teachings in SBJohn. (The notion
of an evil creator, for instance, was loudly decried, even
though some Platonists played with the idea of an errant
cause or wicked world soul.76) Like most Christians think-
ers, however, the author(s) of SBJohn succeeded in mold-
ing Platonism to their own theology. Philosophical ideas
were important tools these Christians applied in the game
of designing, in their time, a still more plausible myth.

                    76
                         Litwa 2021a, 3–11.

                         Irenaeus’ Report
                                     u


Irenaeus wrote his initial account of the “gnostic mob” in
the twenty-ninth chapter of AH book 1 (hereafter 1.29).1
1.29 contains an earlier version of a section later inte-
grated into SBJohn, the section running from the Father
discourse to the creator’s boast. It therefore supplies key
hints as to how SBJohn later came together by a process
of compilation and adaptation.2
   This chapter begins by summarizing 1.29 to lay the
foundation for comparing it with SBJohn III. It draws
attention to other distinct parts of SBJohn that seem to
have been integrated into the text, parts which are not
fully consistent with each other and which show traces of
their preexistence. Included is a reconstruction proposing
how SBJohn was welded and joined together, and how it
developed into the shorter and longer versions that were
translated in Egypt and preserved near Nag Hammadi.


                      The “Barbelo Treatise”
According to 1.29, there is a Father called an Eternity,
unnamable and unageing. This eternity is not alone, but
abides within a “virgin Spirit” called Barbelo. The Father

2
    On this topic, see Marjanen 2019, 237–49.

                         Christ             Incorruptibility

  4 Faculties

                                 Figu r e 6.1

has a Will to manifest himself to Barbelo. Barbelo asks for
three divine and feminine Forms, namely Foreknowledge,
Incorruptibility, and Eternal Life. She then emits a Light
similar to the Father. This Light is anointed and per-
fected. He is called Christ, the beginning of the birth of
all beings of pure intellect. Christ is given three intellec-
tual faculties: Word, Mind, and Will.
   Then the emanations form pairs. Word unites with
Thought, Christ with Incorruptibility, Will with Eternal
Life, and finally Mind with Foreknowledge. Two sets of
five make ten beings – a Decad (or if the Father and Barbelo
are not themselves a pair) an Ogdoad (see Figure 6.1).
   From the union of Word and Thought come Selfborn
and Truth. From Christ and Incorruptibility come the
four Lights, namely Armozel, Raguhel, David, and
Eleleth.3 From Will and Eternal Life come the four fac-
ulties: Grace, Intention, Understanding, and Prudence.

                                  Figu r e 6.3


   Then another sacred marriage occurs. Armozel
unites with Grace, Raguhel with Intention, David with
Understanding, and Eleleth with Prudence – forming an
Ogdoad (see Figure 6.2).
   From the union of the Selfborn and Truth comes
the Perfect Human Adamas, the unconquered one with
invincible power. He marries perfect Gnosis and gains
knowledge of the Father. From their union comes the
tree of gnosis (see Figure 6.3).
   From the union of Armozel and Grace comes Wisdom,
here called “Prunicus” (see Figure 6.4).
   Wisdom lacks a partner and begins seeking one in lower
regions. She acts in simplicity and goodness, but does so
without the Father’s will. Wisdom independently pro-
duces the chief ruler. This ruler takes some of Wisdom’s
power and makes a heavenly sphere to dwell in. Joined
with ignorance – or himself being a manifestation of
Ignorance – this ruler makes powers, angels, heavenly

                                     123
  Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


            Armozel            Grace



                      Wisdom



                                         Chief ruler

                          Figu r e 6.4

spheres, and all things earthly. When he mates with
Daring, he produces five chief vices: Spite, envy, jealousy,
conflict, and lust.
   When mother Wisdom sees what happens, she retreats
above the spheres of the lower world. The creator, left
alone, then boasts that he is a jealous god, and that there
is no other god besides him.
   Thus ends the Barbelo report, a (presumably accurate)
record of what can be named the “Barbelo treatise” which
Irenaeus summarized in the 180s ce.


                       Missing Pieces
The Barbelo treatise materially overlaps with SBJohn
4.2–12.51. The versions, however, are not the same.
The first and most noticeable difference is that Irenaeus’
version lacks SBJohn’s introduction featuring John,
Arimanias, and Jesus. It also apparently lacks the story
of salvation, aka the paradise story (the creation of
Adam and Eve, the birth of Seth, the salvation of earthly
humanity, and so on).
  Of course, Irenaeus could have omitted these sections.
But such large-scale omissions would be uncharacteristic
of his method and counterproductive for his polemical

                               124
                          6 Irenaeus’ Report


purpose. Typically, when Irenaeus set out to summarize a
document, such as Marcus’ lengthy numerological specu-
lations, he quoted or paraphrased the entire text, no mat-
ter how intricate the details.4 Moreover, it was not only
the theogony of SBJohn that threatened Irenaeus, but its
accounts of creation and salvation. In fact, these stories
were even more dangerous to him, since they collided
head on with his ethics and understanding of scripture.
To omit this material would be counterproductive for
Irenaeus, a failure to put arrows in his quiver.
   There are other reasons for thinking that Irenaeus did
not have the full compilation called SBJohn. Irenaeus was
protective of the Gospel of John, and granted it special
authority as the witness of the beloved disciple.5 If he
knew that the author of the Barbelo report presented his
narrative as a revelation of Jesus to John, he would have
tried to refute its claim to apostolic authority. Elsewhere,
Irenaeus had no problem saying that his opponents
appealed to Paul and to other apostles.6 If he knew about
the Johannine introduction to SBJohn, he would have
protested against what he felt to be the wrongful appro-
priation of Johannine authority.
   There is no convincing reason for Irenaeus to have
omitted the story of salvation either. Naturally, Irenaeus
went on to summarize a second report which featured a
story of salvation.7 But this separate story was connected
to and informed by a different theology featuring three
Men and one Woman, not the triad of Father, Barbelo,
and Christ. Irenaeus was well aware that this second

4                             5
    Irenaeus, AH 1.14–16.         Irenaeus, AH 3.11.9.
6                                 7
    Irenaeus, AH 1.8–9, 16.        Irenaeus, AH 1.30.4–13.


                                      125
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


narrative was promoted by a separate group (even if
this group belonged to the same – loosely connected –
“gnostic mob”).8 Apparently, the Barbelo treatise ended,
and Irenaeus then picked up with a second treatise (later
called the “Ophite” report).9
  Accordingly, Irenaeus’ Barbelo treatise probably did not
include the introduction or the story of salvation that are
now part of SBJohn in the longer and shorter versions.10


                     SBJohn III and AH 1.29
SBJohn III presents roughly the same initial story
of God with many of the same characters as in 1.29,
but key differences emerge. In SBJohn III, there is a
much larger section of negative theology describing
the Father, and more glorifying epithets bestowed on
Barbelo. The eternities, according to SBJohn III, do not
form pairs, they do not have children, and they engage
more thoroughly in liturgical praise of the Father and
Barbelon. In SBJohn, Christ and the Selfborn become
the same character. The names of the four Lights are
also different (e.g., Raguhel becomes Oroiael in SBJohn
III; David becomes Daveithe). Adamas in SBJohn
III, furthermore, does not marry perfect Gnosis, and
together they do not produce the tree of gnosis. The
four Lights of SBJohn III are associated with twelve
“eternities,” not four faculties; they house Adamas,
Seth, the seed of Seth, and the late repenters – the latter

 8                            9
     Irenaeus, AH 1.29.1.       Irenaeus, AH 1.30.
10
     Similar conclusions are reached by Waldstein and Wisse 1995, 1;
     Layton 2021; Pleše 2006, 2; Barc and Funk 2012, 38–39.


                                   126
                           6 Irenaeus’ Report


three unmentioned by Irenaeus. According to SBJohn
III, Wisdom does not lack a partner (though ironically
she still seeks for one). Wisdom never descends to the
level of the creator and never retreats back upward (she
remains in the ninth sphere). The creator in SBJohn
III receives the name “Yaldabaoth” and “Saklas,” along
with a luminous cloud and a bestial form (a snake–lion
hybrid). He also makes named authorities and angels in
numerical patterns (twelve and seven). The archontic
names and numbers are missing from Irenaeus’ report,
apart from a passing mention of a lower Ogdoad.
   These differences are substantial, and cannot all be
due to Irenaeus’ omissions or modifications. (Irenaeus’
summaries are not objective, by any means, but they
are not wilful distortions.) Even assuming that Irenaeus
omitted or changed large chunks of material, his ver-
sion was manifestly different from both the shorter and
longer versions of SBJohn as represented by the surviv-
ing Coptic treatises. It seems that Irenaeus was reading
a section of SBJohn before it had been integrated into
SBJohn, since elsewhere he had no problem naming
gospel (or gospel-like) texts (e.g., GJudas, and GTruth).
Irenaeus is thus a key witness to the development of how
a preexisting story about the Father and Wisdom became
part of SBJohn.11

     Thomassen punts on the issue of whether the treatise represented by
     1.29 went on to describe creation and salvation (2023, 29, 115). But,
     he observes, “there are no good reasons to assume that” Irenaeus
     deliberately chose to omit the rest of the story (115). AH 1.29 ends
     with the boast of Yaldabaoth, but the author was aware of a sequel
     (152), which included an anthropogony (149) since the tree of gnosis

                      How SBJohn Emerged
The following reconstruction is based on the earlier data
and scholarly guesswork. The actual historical processes
were doubtless more complex, but some simplification is
necessary in this introductory work.
   Sometime prior to Irenaeus, a Christian theologian
wanted to describe the metaphysical origins of Christ
as a member of the Trinity (Father, Mother, and Son).
To tell this story, he or she was mostly informed by
allegorical readings of the first chapters of Genesis,
Neopythagorean philosophy, and (probably) Valentinian
theology.
   Armed with these intellectual resources, he or she told
a story about the emergence of the Trinity, the marriage
of two Pentads to make a Decad, the Ogdoad of the four
Lights, and their offspring, namely Adamas, Gnosis,
Wisdom, and the first Ruler. This author ended with the
boast of the first Ruler who made the lower angels and
heavens, as well as the earth. There the story stopped.
Apparently our author felt that an account of lower
humanity’s creation and redemption was not necessary
for his or her purposes, even if he or she assumed it.
Irenaeus somehow obtained the author’s “Barbelo trea-
tise,” and reported it in AH 1.29.
   In the late second or third century ce, the author(s)
of SBJohn deemed the story incomplete.12 They wanted

12
     Thomassen (2023, 115) posits an intermediate stage between the
     treatise behind 1.29 and SBJohn. That treatise was used as a source
     for a more extensive treatise that added to it materials taken from
     other sources. It was this expanded treatise that was converted into the
     revelation dialogue of SBJohn. For the details, see Thomassen 2023,
     152.


                                     128
                       6 Irenaeus’ Report


to tell the story of Wisdom’s redemption through the
creation of lower humanity. So they added material after
the boast of Yaldabaoth, leaving behind a literary seam
(between 12.53 and 13.1). From the Father discourse
(4.2) to Yaldabaoth’s boast (12.51), they inscribed a
speech of revelation (a story of God), but after the boast,
they switched to a question-and-answer format in which
an unidentified speaker asks the revealer questions about
the text of Genesis and about salvation (13.1–22.1).
   Three of these questions were woven into the story of
creation. They focused on correcting or modifying what
“Moses” wrote in Genesis:

• “What is this hovering [performed by Wisdom in Gen
  1.2]?” (SBJohn 13.4)
• “Wasn’t it the snake who actually instructed him
  [namely, Adam in Gen 3.1–5]?” (17.16)
• “What is this trance [thrown upon Adam in Gen
  2.21]?” (17.20)

The other seven questions constitute a little “catechism”
on the destiny of souls after death. This “catechism” may
originally have been a separate piece of text woven into
the story of salvation (after Cain and Abel but before the
Flood narrative).13 The seven questions are:
1. “Will the soul of each person be saved in the purity of
   the light?” (SBJohn 20.1)
2. “Those who have not done these [ethical actions],
   what happens to their souls and where do they go?
   Will these into whom the Spirit of life and the power
   comes be redeemed or not?” (20.12–13)

                  13
                       Waldstein 1994, 99–112.


                               129
  Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


3. “The souls of these persons [temporarily overcome by
   the counterfeit spirit], when they come from the flesh,
   where will they go?” (20.20)
4. “Those who have not known the All, what happens to
   their souls or where will they go?” (20.23)
5. “How then does the soul grow small again and get
   planted in a mother’s womb or inside a person?” (20.31)
6. “Those who knew but turned back, what happens to
   their souls and where do they go?” (20.37)
7. “Where did the counterfeit spirit come from?” (21.1).

These two pieces of dialogue probably originally featured
an unnamed disciple speaking with a spiritual master. It
is this spiritual master who retold the paradise story (Gen
2–3), the birth of Cain and Abel (Gen 4), and the tale of
Noah (Gen 6–7). He then answered the questions about
human destiny, defining four types of people:
1. Those guided by the Spirit of life who master their
   passions.
2. Those with the Spirit of life without mastering the
   passions.
3. Those who do not know the All.
4. Those who (did) know, but who rejected that knowledge.

   A composer (or composers) wove together these sev-
eral pieces of text to create a complex narrative, including
a story of God (or the divine world), a story of creation,
and a story of salvation.14
   The same or perhaps other author(s) added Sethite
material to the four Lights section, foreshadowing
the birth of Adam, Seth, and Seth’s seed – figures only

                 14
                      Schenke 2012 [1962], 33–39.


                                130
                            6 Irenaeus’ Report


properly introduced in the story of creation, and not cor-
related with the four types of people in the “little cate-
chism.”15 These author(s) included the idea that believers
enter the third light (Daveithe), while the late repenters
enter the fourth light (Eleleth). They also understood the
Invincible one (i.e., Adamas) as a heavenly Adam, Father of
a heavenly Seth (a model for the earthly Adam and Seth).
They apparently modified the story of Wisdom, giving
her an unnamed partner. But they did not tie together
every loose end. Wisdom still seeks her partner – though,
in the new story, she never had one (SBJohn 10.5).
   The same or other author(s) expanded the negative
theology section of the Father discourse, using a docu-
ment also employed by the author of Allogenes (NHC XI
63,12–25). This section (SBJohn 4.23–33) ran as follows:

He [the Father] is not perfection, he is not blessedness, he is
not divinity, but something superior to these. He is not infinite,
he is not finite, but something superior. He is not bodily, he is
not without a body. He is not large, not small; he has no quan-
tity. He is not a creation or anything like a creation.
   It is entirely impossible to understand him. He is nothing of
entities that exist, but something superior – not as being supe-
rior but because he belongs to himself.
   He does not share in the eternal realms. Time does not exist
for him. For he who shares in eternity is, logically, different
from the one who began to prepare it. No time determines
him, since he does not receive from any other.

By adding this concentrated section of negative theology,
the author(s) of SBJohn further glorified the Father.

15
     Thomassen (2023, 159) points out that the connection of Seth and his
     seed with the four Lights is already in GJudas (49.6 and possibly 52.5).


                                     131
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


   The detail in Irenaeus that Barbelo was “filled with
pleasure at the conception” of her son (1.29.1), was
replaced by the Father’s rejoicing (SBJohn 7.5) at the
birth of his Son. The attendants of Barbelo and of
Christ were never married, and they never produced
the four Lights and the four faculties. The whole idea
of marriage in the upper world was discarded, probably
because the author(s) of SBJohn wanted to promote the
ideal of celibacy.
   The authors converted the four attendants into twelve
eternities not only to act as the heavenly model for the
signs of the zodiac, but also to buffer and defer Wisdom,
making her furthest from the Father.16 Her name and her
position (last of the Dodecad) suggests Valentinian influ-
ence.17 Wisdom becomes the bridgehead between eter-
nity and time; heaven and earth.
   With regard to the story of creation and salvation,
the author(s) retold Genesis 1–7. They probably did not
invent their retelling out of whole cloth. Rather, they had
a document such as that represented by Irenaeus’ second
report on the “gnostic mob,” namely AH 1.30.


                  Building Out the Stories of
                   Creation and Salvation
According to Irenaeus (AH 1.30.6–10), Yaldabaoth
invites his six powers to create earthly humanity and
blows into humanity Mother Wisdom’s dewy light.
Yaldabaoth makes Eve, and Wisdom leads both Adam
and Eve to eat the fruit of knowledge. Adam and Eve are

16                     17
     Logan 1996, 94.        Thomassen 2023, 137.


                                    132
                       6 Irenaeus’ Report


subsequently deprived of Wisdom’s light and expelled
from heaven to earth. Wisdom gives them a whiff of the
power again; they remember their immaterial origin,
give birth to Cain, whom the devil perverts. Yaldabaoth
sends a flood. Wisdom protects Noah, and the earth is
repopulated.
   The author(s) of SBJohn had access to this tale, or some-
thing like it. They adopted some elements of it, adapted
others, and added still more to tell their story of salva-
tion. They adapted the seven designations of the plane-
tary rulers – not agreeing exactly on the names. They also
deployed the same rebuke of Yaldabaoth: “The Human
exists and the Child of the Human!” (14.1). They made
the dewy light into a light power. They emphasized the
Father’s role in sending the Selfborn and the four Lights
to ensure the transfer of power from Yaldabaoth to Adam.
Overall, SBJohn’s author(s) weakened Wisdom’s role.18
Although she is still an agent of salvation, it is primarily
the Father who saves.19
   The author(s) of SBJohn diverged in more ways from
the narrative we find in AH 1.30. According to them,
humans never actually lose the light power. The first
couple are put into earthly bodies before they are driven
from Paradise. Cain is made the child of Yaldabaoth, not
Adam. There is no separate devil called Samael. (Indeed,
Samael becomes a name for Yaldabaoth in the longer ver-
sion of SBJohn.) In SBJohn, Yaldabaoth not only sends a
flood; he also attacks humanity with sinister forces like
deceiving angels, fate, and the counterfeit spirit.

         18
              Thomassen 2023, 40–41, 58.
         19
              Thomassen 2023, 58. Cf. Logan 1996, 216–17.


                               133
  Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


   So by the early third century, it seems, the author(s)
of SBJohn had combined a story of God (in the form of
a revelation discourse), a story of creation (a revision of
Genesis 2–7 in the form of a dialogue), and a story of sal-
vation (including the “little catechism” and the epilogue).
At some point, they added an introduction featuring Jesus
and John. For the sake of consistency, they went back and
made the two dialogue partners throughout the story into
Christ and John. In this way, all of SBJohn became an
apostolic dialogue.
   The shift from human master to Christ is indicated
by another loose end. The originally human revealer
expressed his or her limitations and weakness when it
came to knowing the Father:

What shall I say to you about this unattainable one? He is the
likeness of the light, as far as I can contemplate it. For who
ever understands him? I will say to you as much as I can under-
stand … None of us knows those infinite realities apart from [the
one revealed in him.] He it is who spoke to us. (SBJohn 4.39–47)

The opening, “What will I say?” is reminiscent of
GJohn 12.27, “What will I say? Save me from this
hour?” Nevertheless, the diffidence of this speaker does
not fit Christ’s profile. To be sure, a divine revealer can
ask questions.20 But the speaker here refers to a person
(“the one who revealed him”) who should be Christ,
revealed in the Father, and specifically in the Father’s
bosom (GJohn 1.18). Evidently a finite, human revealer
has been replaced by Christ without updating all the
language.

                20
                     E.g., Corpus Hermeticum 11.16.


                                134
                        6 Irenaeus’ Report


   The intervening author(s) and/or editor(s) of SBJohn
did not smooth over every wrinkle in their narrative, but
they were by no means incompetent. They thoroughly
revised the Barbelo treatise, while at the same time pre-
serving most of its information. They rejected the idea
that the eternities formed pairs – probably because they
rejected the idea that paired aeons (as in Valentinian
thought) legitimated sex and marriage. Accordingly, the
author(s) could not accept the idea, for instance, that
Christ and Incorruptibility united to produce the four
Lights. (But when they narrated the origin of the four
Lights, interestingly, they still mentioned Christ and
Incorruptibility.21)
   Material from the Barbelo treatise was adapted but
also preserved. For instance, the author(s) omitted the
story of Yaldabaoth mating with Audacity to produce
five vices (spite, jealousy, envy, conflict, and greed). But
when describing those endowed with the Spirit of life,
they mentioned freedom from five similar vices (rage,
jealousy, envy, lust, and greed; 20.8).
   This complex editing process explains how SBJohn
came to contain Barbeloite, Sethite, and Ophite mate-
rials.22 The Barbelo theogony (witnessed by Irenaeus,
AH 1.29) was the original core. Then probably came
the stories of creation and salvation (adapting elements
from the story reported in AH 1.30). At some point,
Sethite modifications were made to the story of both
God and salvation to ensure that upper world (the fam-
ily of Adamas) corresponds to the lower world (Adam,
Seth, and Seth’s seed). Finally, a new introduction

21                          22
     Thomassen 2023, 132.        Rasimus 2009, 4, 37, 41.


                                   135
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


added the figure of John, who was also made the
inquirer in the ten questions of the dialogue sections.
The composers of SBJohn were true magpies, pulling
from a host of sources. We might even suppose that
the image of the laughing Jesus in SBJohn was influ-
enced by the Gospel of Judas (probably written in the
mid second century ce).23
   However exactly the threads came together, the shorter
version of SBJohn came into being in Greek, probably in
Alexandria, between 190 and 220 ce.24 A generation later,
perhaps in the same city, another revisor added two other
large sections: The melothesia narrative in the middle
and the Providence monologue at the end (see Chapter
7). This revisor made other thoroughgoing revisions of
SBJohn in light of an updated theology focused on the
saving actions of Providence/Barbelo.
   By about 250 ce, there circulated both a longer and a
shorter version of SBJohn in Greek. The shorter version
underwent further, minor, modifications in the course of
its third- and fourth-century transmission into Coptic,
resulting in two recensions (what we now call the BG
version and the Codex III version). By about 350, the two
recensions of the shorter version and the longer version
had been translated and were inscribed in the first slot of
Nag Hammadi Codices II (longer version), III (shorter
version), and IV (longer version). Another recension
of the shorter version appeared in the second position
of the Berlin codex, transcribed in the fifth century ce.

23
     For the dating, see Brakke 2022, 14. On the theme of laughter, see
     Clivaz 2012, 213–42.
24
     My dating concurs with Burns 2014, 154 and Thomassen 2023, 154,
     156, 159.


                                   136
                    6 Irenaeus’ Report


The longer version never replaced the shorter, just as the
Gospels according to Matthew and Luke never replaced
Mark. This point indicates that the shorter version had
attained a kind of scriptural status by the third or fourth
century and was used by one or more Christian commu-
nities in Egypt.




                           137
                                    7
                     The Myth Received
                                    u


One cannot fully understand a text without grasping how
it was understood by its initial readers. For this task, one
has the tools of reception history – the history of the
interpretation of a text. The first documentable reception
of the shorter version (here represented by SBJohn III) is
the longer version (here represented by SBJohn in Codex
II). The reception of SBJohn in other Nag Hammadi and
related literature is not a matter of citation and quotation,
but of piecemeal appropriation and adaptation.


                    From Shorter to Longer
SBJohn II is more similar to SBJohn III than it is differ-
ent. In fact, it seems that many of the changes in the lon-
ger version are rooted in differences of oral performance
(alternative phrasing of the same content for rhetorical
effect).1 At the same time, the editor(s) of SBJohn II evi-
dently wanted to clarify certain matters in the shorter ver-
sion and to remove inconsistencies and repetitions. For
instance, the shorter version identifies Insight with the
tree of knowledge and the eagle roosting on it, whereas
the longer version identifies the eagle with another char-
acter, namely Christ.2

1
    King 1997, 105–37.
2
    Another example: The confusion and obscurity of the shorter versions
    (18.13–15) is cleared up in the longer version (II 61,17–62,7).


                                   138
                         7 The Myth Received


   In general, SBJohn II intensifies the Christian features
of the text. The tendency is seen in the title itself. It is
no longer the Secret Book of John but according to John
(SBJohn II, 83,8),3 a title which resembles the Gospel
according to John and more clearly makes the Secret Book
a gospel. Other Christian elements are evident. The
Father is said to give mercy and salvation (II 9,1–2), in
accordance with Pauline passages like 2 Corinthians 1.3.
The Selfborn God is given the name above every name
(II 18,17) – a name given to Christ in Philippians 2.9.
Christ, not Luminous Insight, is the one who appears as
an eagle on the tree of knowledge (II 62,15). The name of
Jesus Christ, along with a ceremonial “Amen,” is added at
the end of the text (II 83,7).
   The editor(s) of SBJohn II also nuanced the portraits of
the Father and Mother. Everything is said to exist in the
Father (II 5,18). He – not Barbelo – is repeatedly called
“virginal” Spirit (II 13,2; 15,18; 18,7). His role as a direct
actor in the story is, however, diminished. The chief actor
becomes Barbelo (II 51,13) – spelled without a final “n” –
whose titles are expanded and to some degree routinized.
She is called the “womb of everything” (II 11,19), the
“mother–father” (II 12,1; 15,14), and holy Spirit (II 12,3).
Her identity as Providence is more closely guarded inso-
far as other beings called Providence are renamed (e.g., II
20,4; 40,3). Barbelo is the direct image of the Father, and
her role as the model of the animate Adam is underscored.
She explicitly becomes the archetype of the human image
that appears in the waters (II 37,16).

3
    References to SBJohn II are to the synoptic edition. See the Note at
    the start of Chapter 2.


                                    139
  Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


   Sex, sex appeal, and sexual desire are more closely
aligned with the feminine in SBJohn II. Instead of sex-
ual desire being implanted in Adam (II 65,8), in SBJohn
II it is planted in Eve – who is named “the one who
belonged to Adam.” Eve is also shown preparing her-
self for her husband (II 63,5) – a scene with some sexual
valence, since in the next sentence Yaldabaoth – or,
less likely, Adam – is “lord” over her.4 The longer ver-
sion also omits the observation that male domination
in marriage was part of Yaldabaoth’s curse (II 63,6) –
leaving one to infer that male headship in marriage is
natural.
   Several modifications lead one to think that the
author(s) of SBJohn II were more androcentric than
those of SBJohn III. After all, the Father, not Barbelo,
bears the Selfborn in SBJohn II (II 15,4); accordingly,
the Selfborn is not son of Barbelo, but of the Father (II
18,3), like Adam is the father of Seth. The imprint of the
true Human above is male in form, according to SBJohn
II, not that of a generic human (II 37,20). This is ironic,
since if the true Human is Barbelo, the form of humanity
ought to be both male and female.
   The author(s) of SBJohn III are insistent that the rul-
ers built from Wisdom’s image alone. They did not use
their own likeness, despite the fact that they used their
own qualities to build Adam’s soul. In the longer version,
however, the rulers make Adam “after their own likeness,”
which means that a part of Adam’s soul did manifest his
archontic ancestry. The lower soul is not redeemed; only
the higher mind.

              4
                  See, in general, King 1988, 158–76.


                                140
                   7 The Myth Received


   SBJohn II also intensifies the role of fate, naming it
“bitter” and attributing it to the union of Yaldabaoth
and the lowest of his authorities (Wisdom). Fate is called
“hard and strong,” the “last of the changeable fetters.”
From fate comes every sin, injustice, blasphemy, forget-
fulness, ignorance, severe commands, and great fears. By
it all creation is blinded (II 75,6–16) – with the exception
of the immovable family.
   The two most obvious additions made by the editor(s)
of SBJohn II are the section on melothesia (the angels who
arranged Adam’s animate body parts), and the Providence
hymn. SBJohn III relates that 360 angels fused the
joints and limbs of the animate Adam. SBJohn II names
seventy-two of these angels, along with many other powers
who control the vital processes and qualities of the body:

• Seven powers create seven souls (II 15,13–29).
• Seventy-two angels make Adam’s animate body, over-
  seen by the seven (II 15,29–17,8).
• Thirty angels are active in Adam’s animate limbs (II
  17,8–32).
• Five angels are put in charge of Adam’s vital processes
  (II 17,32–18,2).
• Four demons take control of Adam’s material qualities
  (II 18,2–14).
• Four demons rule Adam’s passions (II 18,14).5

These lists of angels and/or demons seem to have been
compiled from several sources. At least one of the sources
was a book attributed to Zoroaster, to which the editor(s)
of SBJohn II refer (II 50,20). Alternatively, this book of

                    5
                        Waldstein 1994, 101.


                              141
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


Zoroaster contained material of the same sort as what is
found in the melothesia. The book was probably an astro-
logical tractate of Egyptian inspiration, since exactly
seventy-two angels who make the animate body are twice
thirty-six, the number of the Egyptian decans.6 (Porphyry
also noted that gnostics in his vicinity used an apocalypse
ascribed to Zoroaster, and Prodican Christians report-
edly used books of Zoroaster as well.7)
   It is sometimes stated that the melothesia section is
about healing. The editor(s) of SBJohn connected names
to demons because control of the demon meant control
of the body part.8 It is possible that readers of SBJohn
invoked demonic names, but in the surviving text there
is no evidence of them using them for healing.9 Celsus
claimed that Egyptians linked human body parts to the
thirty-six daimones (that is, decans) and invoked them
for healing.10 But these Egyptians are not said to be
gnostic or Christian. Only Plotinus claims that his pre-
sumably Christian opponents (whom Porphyry called
“gnostics”) claimed to purify themselves of sicknesses.
They identified the sicknesses with demons and claimed
to drive them out by a “word” (not necessarily a demonic
name).11 Expertise in the knowledge of demonic names
was probably good in itself, whether or not it led to
bodily healing.


 6
     On the decans, see SH 6 in Litwa 2018, 55; Quack 1995, 97–122.
 7
     Porphyry, Life of Plotinus 16; Clement, Miscellanies 1.15.69.6.
 8
     King 2006, 112–13; Adamson 2013, 333–58; Paulsen 2022.
 9
     Adamson (2013, 351) proposes that the users of SBJohn invoked
     demonic superiors to thwart demonic inferiors.

     Plotinus, Enneads 2.9.14 (LCL 441.279).


                                   142
                    7 The Myth Received


  The other addition is the providence hymn, placed at
the end of SBJohn. There is perhaps no more beautiful
passage in all of gnostic literature. Here the voice of the
Mother resounds:
It is I who am the Providence, the completion of the All. I
changed into my seed, for I existed in the beginning. I was trav-
eling every road, for I am the richness of the Light. It is I who
am the thought of the Fullness. I walked in the vastness of the
dark and I endured until I went into the midst of the prison.
Then the foundations of chaos shook. It is I who hid myself in
them on account of their evil, and no one knew me.
   Again I returned a second time. I proceeded to emerge
from those who belong to the light – I, the active thought of
Providence – I went into the midst of the dark, to the inner
rind of hell, pursuing my plan. Then the foundations of chaos
shook so as to fall upon and destroy those who dwell in chaos.
So again I ran up to my luminous root so that their destruction
(might not occur) before the time.
   Once more for the third time I went – I, the light existing in
the Light. It is I, the active thinking of Providence. To enter
the inner rind of the dark, the inside of hell, I filled my face
with light in the completion of their region. I went into the
midst of their prison, the prison of the body.
   Then I said: the one who hears, rise from deep sleep. He
wept and shed tears, heavy tears he wiped from himself and
said, “Who calls my name? From where does this hope come
to me, since I dwell in the chain of the prison?”
   Then I said: “It is I, the Providence of the pure light! It is
I, the thinking of the virgin Spirit, who draws you to an hon-
ored place. Rise and activate your thought, for you have heard.
Follow your root, which is I, the compassionate, and guard
yourself from the angels of poverty, and the demons of chaos,
all those who entangle you. Wake up from deep sleep and from
the straitjacket of inner hell.”

                              143
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature

  I lifted him up and sealed him in the light of the water of the
Five Seals so that death might have no power over him from
that time on. (II 79,5–82,1)

The addition of the Providence hymn at the end of
SBJohn II affected other parts of the text as well.12 In
brief, the story of creation and salvation in SBJohn
was reshaped so that it fits three descents of Barbelo: A
descent to model Adam’s creation, a descent to appear
as Luminous Insight, and a full incarnation in Jesus. The
image of rising from sleep in the hymn appears in the
main text of SBJohn II when the eagle roosts on the tree
of knowledge. The hymn also makes mention of the spe-
cific Sethian baptismal rite of the Five Seals, a rite not
mentioned in the shorter version.
   Strictly speaking, the expansionary revision of SBJohn
tells us only about the theology of its editor(s), not of
their larger Christian network. At the same time, one
could infer that these editor(s) wanted to preserve and
reshape SBJohn for a Christian circle that continued to
exist during the first half of the third century ce. Mention
of a communal rite (the Five Seals), a focus on Seth’s
“seed” (which presumably refers to members of a group),
and the revision of inconsistencies suggests that SBJohn
II belonged to a group which was concerned to defend
the logic of its own master narrative, to honor its divine
Father while at the same time focusing its worship on
their divine Mother.
   The edits do not reveal a group embittered by heresi-
ological attacks, or estranged from Christianity.13 In fact,
the updater(s) of SBJohn highlighted elements of their

12                                      13
     Barc and Painchaud 1999, 317–33.        Turner 2007, 908.


                                  144
                       7 The Myth Received


Christian ideology and identity. Barbelo is the divine
Mother who has lost none of her powers – but she is also
Jesus, and Jesus is evidently her supreme manifestation.
Apparently, the users of SBJohn grew more focused on
Christ, not less.


         The Reception of SBJohn in Other Texts
SBJohn is often taken to be the quintessential expression
of “the gnostic myth,”14 a myth which presumably influ-
enced all its later retellings. For this reason, the creator
of the modern term “Sethian” once contemplated call-
ing Sethian texts the “Apocryphon of John-group.”15
Tracing direct lines of influence is never clear cut, but
it does seem that the Hypostasis of the Archons (NHC
II,4) develops SBJohn’s demonology and anthropology,
while the Holy Book (NHC II,2; IV,2) and Trimorphic
Protennoia (NHC XIII,1) adapts its triple descent scheme
and Five Seals ritual.16
   One might suppose that any text combining an extensive
negative theology, the figure of Adamas, the four Lights,
the Selfborn, and Barbelo was influenced by SBJohn. But
the devil is in the details, and it remains challenging to
prove that a set of features or characters came distinctly
from SBJohn as opposed to other “Sethian” texts and
oral lore. Sethian texts belong to a kind of intertextual
echo chamber, so identifying actual historical lines of
influence between texts is difficult. One might assume,
based on its fourfold preservation, that SBJohn was more

14
     Layton-Brakke 2021, 25. 15 Schenke 1981, 503.
16
     Logan 2012, 136, n.4. Cf. Logan 1996, 55.


                                 145
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


authoritative than other texts in the “Sethian” category,
and repeated copying is the best sign of a widespread
reception.
   The following examples only indicate a possible use
of SBJohn. A fragmentary second-century meditation
on creation called the Berlin Coptic Book (P20915)
mentions “Sethians.” It names Yaldabaoth as the great
ruler who created humanity along with six angelic cre-
ators: Sabaoth, Adonaios, [Yaoth], Eloaios, Oraios, and
[Astaphaios] (128). Four of these names resemble what
we find in SBJohn (12.42–48): Yaoth, Eloaios, Astaphaios,
and Sabaoth, but there is no exact overlap. In fact, the
names in the Berlin Coptic Book are closer to those
found in Irenaeus’s AH 1.30.17 No direct influence from
SBJohn is necessary (in fact, direct influence would be
impossible if SBJohn was written in the third century).
   The Letter of Peter to Philip mentions Mother
(Wisdom)’s foolishness, a “part” she left behind, the
“arrogant one” who grabbed it, sowed it, and confined it
in the mortal realms, along with the powers who molded
mortal bodies based on the appearance that had come
forth (135,10–12).18 Similarly, the Hypostasis of the
Archons features a boast from Yaldabaoth, a voice from
heaven, an image on the waters, and the molding of an
initially inert Adam.
   Much of this material, though it sounds like what we
find in SBJohn, did not necessarily come from SBJohn
directly. “The” gnostic myth was not ever a standardized


17
     Irenaeus, AH 1.30.5; cf. Origen, Against Celsus. 6.31; Orig World
     (NHC II,5) 101.29–35. See further Rasimus 2009, 57–58.
18
     Marjanen 2006, 567–81.


                                   146
                   7 The Myth Received


or singular entity. Like all myths, it was variable and
alive. Accordingly, one could posit the direct influence of
SBJohn on these texts. Alternatively, one might imagine a
web of other, intervening texts or oral traditions as more
directly influencing these texts.
   Another example is Zostrianos. This text names specific
characters like the “Selfborn,” “Adamas” (NHC VIII,1
6,8; 6,26), the “four Lights” (29,1), “Barbelo” (36,20),
and the “Invisible Spirit” (24,9–10). But, once again,
the mention of distinctive characters in SBJohn do not
prove that the author of Zostrianos used SBJohn directly.
Nevertheless, textual features like Wisdom given a place
of rest after her repentance (10,8–9), a dialogue on souls
saved and lost (42,16–44,22), a Father discourse (64,14–
65,23), and a long disquisition on Barbelo (76,2–81,20)
signal the probable use of SBJohn, though Zostrianos
is, in the end, significantly different in content and filled
with new and altered characters.
   Trimorphic Protennoia mentions a triad of divine per-
sons – Father, Mother, and Son (NHC XIII 37,22) – and
a triad of the creator’s names (Yaldabaoth, Saklas, and
Samael, 39,27). First Thought calls herself “Barbelo,” the
“perfect glory,” “image of the Invisible Spirit,” Mother,
and Virgin (38,7–16). She is Mother and Father (45,3),
as well as womb of the all (45,6–7). Close resemblance
in language and content leads one to believe that the
Providence hymn in the longer version of SBJohn influ-
enced Trimorphic Protennoia. According to Trimorphic
Protennoia, Barbelo reaches chaos, hides in her own peo-
ple, gives them shape, reveals herself to the children of
light (40,29–41,1), breaks the chains of netherworldly
demons (41,4–6), shakes chaos (43,9–10), and arrives a

                            147
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


third time in human form (47,13–14). All of these actions
Barbelo performs in the Providence hymn.19 Some con-
nection is likely, although the author of Trimorphic
Protennoia could have encountered the Providence hymn
independently of SBJohn.
   The Holy Book of the Great Invisible Spirit is another
variant of the Sethian master story. It mentions the
Trinity of Father–Mother–Child, the Great Invisible
Spirit, Barbelo as a male virgin, the Four Lights (featur-
ing Harmozel not Armozel, as in SBJohn III), Adamas,
Seth, the twelve archons (whose names better align with
the longer version), the boast of Saklas and its response
(“Humanity exists and the Child of Humanity”).20 In
other ways, the Sethian myth in the Holy Book is so dif-
ferent from SBJohn that content in the Holy Book might
well be independent and earlier than SBJohn.
   The Second Discourse of Great Seth (NHC VII,2) dis-
tinctively mentions “our fellow sister Wisdom” (50,27) as
she is found in SBJohn (10.1; 16.9). The archons are dis-
turbed by Christ’s incarnation (VII 51,20–31; 54,24–27),
much like what we see in the Providence hymn. The idea
that Moses was a “joke” (VII 63,26) was perhaps inspired
by Jesus’ chuckling at the naivety of Moses’ creation story
(e.g., SBJohn 13.5; 17.17).
   A section of negative theology in Allogenes (62,28–
63,25) may depend on SBJohn, or both texts may depend
on an independent third source. (Extended negative the-
ologies had become fashionable in the second and third
centuries ce.21)

19
     Poirier 2010, 93–103. 20 Holy Book 41–42, 49, 51, 52, 58–59, 61.
21
     Hancock 1992, 167–86; Williams 2000, 277–302.


                                  148
                        7 The Myth Received


   Erin Evans points out the influence of SBJohn on the
Second Book of Jeu. She notes that the author of this text
inserted the Father–Mother–Child triad in the twelfth
level of twelve material aeons.22 On the thirteenth level
appears a “great invisible god” and a “great virgin spirit,”
whom Evans understands to refer to the Invisible, virginal
Spirit, split into two entities.23 The figure of Yaldabaoth
is also “slotted in” the third aeon as one of “numerous
lower rulers set to bar the path of the uninitiated during
ascent.”24
   What Evans calls “Pistis Sophia 4a” deals with the
punishment of souls in a place called the “middle realm”
and what it calls the baptism of the “first offering.” In
it, Barbelo is demoted to a being with a material nature.
She dwells in the thirteenth aeon and provides Jesus’
material body.25 She is involved in Wisdom’s ema-
nation as Wisdom’s mother, and works to repair her
deficiency.26
   Pistis Sophia 4b largely concerns the punishment of
various categories of sinners (thieves, murderers, the
proud, and so on). Here Yaldabaoth and his forty-nine
minions often appear as agents of punishment.27 The
tradition of Yaldabaoth as an evil creator was too wide-
spread, however, to confirm a direct use of SBJohn.
   The third book of Pistis Sophia focuses on the vices and
virtues and the need for patience and compassion on sin-
ners. Here we find numerous mentions of the “counterfeit

22
     Evans 2015, 58.
23
     Evans 2015, 58–59, referring to 2BkJeu B45 (83) 17–18.
24
     Evans 2015, 60–61. 25 Evans 2015, 125. 26 Evans 2015, 126.
27
     E.g., Pistis Sophia 144, 146–47 (Schmidt-MacDermott 1978, 375,
     378–80); Evans 2015, 150.


                                  149
     Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


spirit,” which the archons use to catalyze sin.28 In both Pistis
Sophia and SBJohn, “the counterfeit spirit is designed as
‘the enemy of the soul’ (as it compels the soul to succumb
to passions and to sin continually).”29
   If Pistis Sophia is here dependent on SBJohn, it devel-
ops the notion of the counterfeit spirit in its own way.30
It is not concerned with a broad scheme of salvation his-
tory, but only with the individual soul in life and after
death. According to Pistis Sophia, the counterfeit spirit
is not introduced by archons, but naturally embedded
in humanity. Pistis Sophia presents salvation in terms of
knowledge and initiation, whereas SBJohn requires the
descent of a redeemer and Luminous Insight.31
   Wisdom’s story in Pistis Sophia 1–2 is analogous but
different from her story in SBJohn. Child of the invisible
God, and dwelling in the thirteenth aeon, Pistis Sophia
sees the “light of the veil of the Treasury of Light” and
desires to go there. She begins singing praises and ceases
to perform her “mystery.” The lower rulers, led by
“Authades” (Daring), grow to hate her. They persecute
Wisdom, and her matter in chaos becomes a lion-faced
power called Yaldabaoth.32 This is a story of Wisdom’s
fall, but it is significantly different from what we find in
SBJohn.

28
     Pistis Sophia 3.111 (Schmidt-MacDermott 281–83); 3.131–32
     (Schmidt-MacDermott 137–39); Evans 2015, 174.

                        7 The Myth Received


   According to Evans, Pistis Sophia’s story is “an adap-
tation – and subversion – of the version of Sophia’s fall
and restoration” in SBJohn.33 She is an offspring of the
invisible God, though she is “reduced from a divine aeon
to a confused material being.”34 She has no partner and
she does not want to imitate her Father. Also, unlike her
character in SBJohn, Pistis Sophia is passive, and her
lengthy journey of repentance is not connected to the
history of salvation.35
   From this evidence, Evans intuits a polemic against
SBJohn and against Sethian tradition more generally.
“The message is, Sethian beliefs and practices are not divine,
and cannot save you.” Wisdom is not a divine power; she
is ignorant and powerless and “does not impart a con-
nection to the true height.”36 Evans believes that, in the
third- or fourth-century Egyptian milieu, “Sethians”
posed a threat to the “Jeuians” who composed the books
of Jeu and Pistis Sophia, leading to a “polemic against
their primary mythological figures” who were demoted
to the material realm.37
   Whether or not we see a polemic against SBJohn in
the Books of Jeu and Pistis Sophia, it is plain that their
composers – if they used SBJohn directly – employed it
in a loose and perhaps highhanded way, reshaping it for
their own purposes. They made little attempt to preserve
SBJohn’s integrity or storyline; instead, they took what
they wanted and revised it according to a new scheme.
   But this stance toward SBJohn is not unique; in fact,
revisionary license is a general characteristic of SBJohn’s

33                      34                      35
     Evans 2015, 237.        Evans 2015, 238.        Evans 2015, 241.
36                      37
     Evans 2015, 245.        Evans 2015, 137.


                                     151
  Part III Relationship to Other Early Christian Literature


reception. Most readers of SBJohn, it seems, did not con-
sider it an unrevisable text of supreme authority. It was
not “Bible,” gnostic or otherwise. Characters and ter-
minology were recycled and fitted into a new – often a
more luxuriant – mythology with many more characters,
heavenly levels, and narrative details. SBJohn was just
another stepping stone to the true revelation, which only
an elite literary expert could remember. Readers – be they
Sethian or Jeuian – transformed the story, making it hard
to recognize the antecedent text.
   It seems reasonable, then, to distinguish a “Sethian”
literary category from the Sethian Christian circle
which used SBJohn as an authoritative text. SBJohn and
other “Sethian” texts (like Zostrianos, Allogenes, and
the Three Steles of Seth) might share many themes
and characters, but they did not necessarily belong
to the same sociological group or social identity. The
authors of the Books of Jeu and Pistis Sophia were cer-
tainly not part of a Sethian circle. If Evans is right, they
competed with it. Quite possibly several early Christian
groups claimed to be the seed of Seth and the immov-
able family – and not all of these groups were friendly
toward one another.


                       Conclusion
To sum up, SBJohn was very probably read in Egypt
between the third and fifth centuries ce. Those who
respected SBJohn’s authority updated the text as they saw
fit, expanded it, and tried to smooth out its contradic-
tions. When SBJohn migrated beyond the confines of its
own community, however, outside readers did not treat

                            152
                       7 The Myth Received


it as authoritative scripture. Instead, they pirated, trans-
formed, and sometimes parodied its stories.
   Such reception is similar to the reception of Greek
myth among the great dramatists and ancient poets (for
instance, Euripides and Ovid). There was no truly canon-
ical version of Greek myth. Even if Homer was author-
itative, he was often incomplete and undetailed in his
telling of myth. Thus elite writers pulled from a variety
of sources, which they did not cite, and reshaped myths
according to their own designs and purposes. This was
the normal way to receive myth in antiquity, for what
mattered was the myth, not any textual instantiation of it.
   Eventually the myth – at least as recorded in SBJohn –
was forgotten. The latest copy of SBJohn that survives
(BG) is from the fifth century. After the fifth century, it
seems, SBJohn fell into disuse and its readership disap-
peared. Thankfully, four copies of SBJohn were hidden
away under different circumstances and rediscovered in
the late nineteenth and mid twentieth centuries. The
exact details of their rediscovery are still contested, and
many questions remain unanswered.38
   At present, however, SBJohn – now translated into all
manner of modern languages – has hundreds, if not thou-
sands, of readers worldwide. It has even been accepted
into living gnostic (and gnostic Christian communities).39
A more thorough study of the ancient and modern recep-
tions of SBJohn would be a fascinating and welcome topic
for exploration.

38
     Robinson 2014; Goodacre 2013, 303–22; Denzey Lewis 2014,
     399–419; Burns 2016, 5–11.
39
     Dillon 2017, 199–223.

Note: The following is not a commentary on SBJohn III,
but covers only aspects of the text not fully addressed in
the preceding chapters.1

1 John’s Turn to the Temple
1.1 one of these days. One of the days after Jesus’ resurrec-
tion and ascension. Compare the setting of, for instance,
SBJames 2,7–3,38; Wisdom of Jesus Christ 90,14–93,24.2
   when he went up to the temple. SBJohn assumes that the
apostle John initially went up to the temple and partic-
ipated in the worship of the Judean deity. Compare the
setting of GLuke 24.52–53 and Acts 3.1. Jesus’ disciples
after his ascension remained in Jerusalem and participated
in Jewish temple rites, which indicate their involvement in
the practices and institutions of Jewish life. Participating
in Jewish rites like kosher laws, Sabbath, and circumcision
may have been the practice of many devotees of Jesus in
the first century ce, but this was not the case after the
second century.3 See the note on “I turned aside from the
temple” in SBJohn 2,1.


1
    For commentaries, see Giversen 1963a, Tardieu 1984, and Bream
    1987, 3–162.
2
    See further Hartenstein 2000. 3 Litwa 2024a, 55–65.


                                  155
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


  a Pharisee … Arimanias. The vilification of the Pharisees
continues the Synoptic and Johannine trajectory.4
Arimanias (aka Ahriman) is the Greek name for the evil
principle in Zoroastrian theology, opposite to Oromasdes.5
The reader is thus primed to think something sinister
about him.

1.2 Where is your teacher …? “Teacher” (ⲥⲁϩ) can also mean
“master.” Jesus was a teacher in the Gospels (e.g., GMark
9.38; GJohn 3.2). Arimanias’ question seems to assume that
Jesus was alive somewhere – that he did not really die and
therefore his resurrection was a fraud.

1.4 This Nazorean utterly deceived you. Jesus is called
“Nazorean” at his arrest (GJohn 18.5, 7) and sentencing
(19.19; cf. GMatthew 2.23; GMark 14.67). “Nazorean”
became the standard Jewish way to refer to “Christian”
in antiquity.6 Arimanias’ criticism resembles that against
Christians in Celsus’ speech of the Jew (Origen, Against
Celsus 1.28–2.79). Celsus’ Jew reportedly spoke to Jewish
believers in Jesus, saying that they have been “deluded
by Jesus, they have left the law of their fathers, and have
been quite ludicrously deceived.”7 If the speech of Celsus’
Jew does not go back to an actual Jewish document, it
does seem to represent authentic Jewish criticisms of
Christians in mid to late second-century Alexandria.8

    Philosophers 1.8.

    Epiphanius, Panarion 29.1.3; 29.6.2–3; Jerome, Epistle 112.13. See also

   The closing of the heart telescopes the language of
Isaiah 6.10 LXX (later adapted in SBJohn 17.23): “for the
heart of this people has grown thick; they heard with dif-
ficulty with their ears and their eyes have closed.”

1.5 The traditions of the ancestors generally refer to Jewish
rites and customs such as food laws and circumcision.9

2 John’s Questions in the Desert
2.1 I turned aside from the temple. This act seems to imply
John’s broader rejection of Jewish rites, customs, and
place(s) of worship.10 Such rejection became character-
istic of Christians in Alexandria after 117 ce, and is wit-
nessed in Alexandrian writings such as the Preaching of
Peter and the Epistle of Barnabas.11

2.2–3 I was grieving heavily. Opposition and grief were
commonly experienced among the prophets who were
rejected by the officials of the established religion. The
Book of Lamentations, for instance, was widely attributed
to the persecuted prophet Jeremiah.
   How then …? John’s cosmological and theological ques-
tions (not raised by Arimanias) indicate that the Savior’s
earthly teaching was incomplete – not just in need of
clarification. Compare the questions in Wisdom of Jesus
Christ (NHC III,4 90,14–92,3) and Letter of Peter to
Philip (NHC 7.2 134,23–135,2).12 For the idea that lower
world corresponds to higher, see Chapter 5.


 9
     GMark 7.3, 5; GMatthew 15.3; Gal 1.14; Josephus, Jewish Antiquities
     13.297; Ptolemy, Letter to Flora 4.12.
10
     Adamson 2016, 36–55. 11 Litwa 2024a, 66–88.
12
     Luttikhuizen 1988, 158–68.
