3 Jesus Revealed
3.1–2 the heavens opened … I was terrified. Compare GJohn
1.51, “You will see heaven opened.” The language is
common when describing prophetic ecstasy. The heavens
open in Jesus’ baptismal vision in GMatthew 3.16; GLuke
3.21; Stephen sees the heavens open before his death in
Acts 7.55, and the door of heaven opens in Rev 4.1 (cf.
19.11). Blinding light, earthquake, and the response of
fear are classic signs of epiphany – the bursting of a divin-
ity into the earthly sphere.13

3.3–4 Jesus’ appearance as a child is reminiscent of the
appearance of Jesus to Valentinus as attested in the
Refutation of All Heresies 6.42.2: “Valentinus claims
that he saw a child, an infant just recently born. When he
inquired to find out who he was, the child replied that he
was the Logos.”14 The shift from child to a luminous old
man indicates that John did not see Jesus at the lowest level
of revelation; he had reached the heights of spiritual matu-
rity and saw Jesus as Daniel beheld the Ancient of Days in
Dan 7.9.
   whether it was [a woman]. Till, Schenke, and Pleše restore
woman (ⲥϩⲓⲙⲉ), not “likeness” (ϩⲓⲇⲉⲁ) like Waldstein-
Wisse and Funk.15 The restoration of “woman” is sup-
ported internally on two grounds: (1) The lacuna requires
a feminine noun; (2) The three faces (old man, woman, and
child) correspond to Christ’s own proclamation that he


13
     See further Litwa 2014, 111–40. Further parallels in van Unnik 1964,
     269–80.
14
     Cf. GJudas 33.20.
15
     Till and Schenke 1972, 82; Pleše, 2006, 28–40. See also King 2011,
     527, n.30.


                                   158
     Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


is the Father, the Mother, and the Son in SBJohn 3.9. The
Jesus who appears in multiple forms is most similar to Acts
of John 87–105 (see Chapter 3).

3.5 why, doubleminded …? In an epiphany, the god or angel
will often first try to allay fear. For the command not to
be doubleminded, compare, for instance, the Shepherd of
Hermas, Visions 2.13.7; 3.3.4; 4.1.4.

3.6 you are no stranger to this likeness. Jesus never
self-​identifies in the vision. Still, he assumes that John is
familiar with him. Compare John in Acts of John 88.1,
who addresses those who have seen Jesus in the form of
a child and in the form of John himself: “You have expe-
rienced nothing strange or incredible in your perception
of the Lord.”

3.8 I who am with you. A common epiphanic reassurance.
Compare GMatthew 28.20. “Behold I am with you all the
days;” Corpus Hermeticum 1.2 (the voice of Poimandres):
“I am with you everywhere.”

3.9 Father … Mother … Child. Jesus’ identification with
all three primary manifestations of Godhead (later named
Invisible Spirit, Barbelon, and Selfborn) indicates a kind
of modalism that was prevalent in the early third century
in Rome and other places. We know of these modalis-
tic trends chiefly from those who attacked them, such as
the Refutator (Refutation 9.7.1–9.12.26) and Tertullian
(Against Praxeas). The idea that the Trinity resembles
a nuclear family with a female manifestation (Mother)
is a Sethian distinctive. Logos Christians like Justin and
Tatian typically emphasized a Father–Son (or God–
Logos) binitarianism.

                            159
     Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


  Silke Petersen argues that the Christ who appears
in SBJohn is an unstable figure, in view of his age and
appearance as well as in his sexual identity: He pres-
ents himself as “Mother” and is identified with a female
revealer, Providence.16
3.10 I am he who exists forever. Here Jesus sounds most like
his character in Revelation 1.8 (“I am the Alpha and the
Omega”) and 1.17 (“I am the first and the last”).
3.11 what is … what was … what must come to pass. Whereas
one might expect a chronological order (“what was, is,
and is to come”), Jesus in SBJohn begins Platonically with
what Is, the truly real.17 At the same time, there is perhaps
an echo of Revelation 1.4. “the one who is, was, and will
come.” Compare Homer, Iliad 1.70: Calchas the prophet
knew “things present, things to come, and things prior.”
The Muses in Hesiod’s Theogony 38 unite their voices
to speak “things present, things future, and things past.”18
   The perfect Human could refer to Barbelon, the Selfborn,
or Adamas – or all three at once. It is not a phrase that typ-
ically appears in other Gospels, but it resonates with verses
from Pauline writings such as Ephesians 4.13, “Until all
attain … to the perfect man (ἄνδρα τέλειον) to the mea-
sure … of Christ’s fullness.” For Christ as perfected (or
mature), see also Hebrews 2.10. “It was fitting that God …
should make the pioneer of their salvation perfect.”
3.12 immovable family. Although some translators have
preferred “race” instead of “family” when translating

      16
           Petersen 2022, 61–78.
      17
           Plato, Timaeus 37e–38a. See further Pleše 2006, 43–47.
      18
           See further van Unnik 1963, 86–94.


                                  160
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


ⲅⲉⲛⲉⲁ, the idea of “family” underscores notions of fluidity
and inclusive membership. Anyone can become part of
the immovable family because it is not based on genes, but
gnosis. Immovable “generation” is possible as well, but
not ideal, because “generation” in common English has
the sense of a delimited period of time in which humans
are born (such as “Generation X”). One can become part
of the immovable family in any generation.19

4 The Father’s Discourse
4.1 I asked to understand. In SBJohn, deeper understanding
relates to the philosophical questions posed in 2.2–3. The
request to understand the nature of God and the world
echoes the Hermetic initiate in Poimandres (Corpus
Hermeticum 1.3): “I wish to learn about the things that
are, to understand their nature and to know God” (trans.
Copenhaver, modified).

4.2 The One is a singular ruler. The One or “Monad” is a
Pythagorean designation for the ultimate principle of the
universe. Literally, Jesus says that the One is a “monarch.”
This is not a typical philosophical way of describing God.
Ancient Jews and Christians (e.g., Philo, Justin Martyr,
Tatian, Theophilus) argued that God was a monarch often as
an apologetic maneuver against Hellenic polytheism.20 But
the term “monarch” for God is also found in the Hermetica.21

4.3 He is God and the Father. “Father” is equally a Christian
and a Platonic epithet for God (Plato, Timaeus 28c; Eph


19
     For the theme of the immovable family in Nag Hammadi literature,
     see Williams 1985.
20
     McCashen 2022, 621–26. 21 E.g., Stobaean Hermetica 23.30, 69.


                                  161
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


4.6) Holiness is distinctively rooted in Jewish language
(e.g., Isa 6.3). The other epithets (dealing with invisibility,
incorruptibility, luminosity), though consisting of biblical
taglines, are compatible with Platonic understandings.22

4.7 One must not think of him as a god. The author(s) of SBJohn
did not deny that the prime reality could be called God (4.3);
but this primal reality was far above what Platonists called
“born gods,” or gods who came into being from more pri-
mal reality.23 In SBJohn, born gods include Barbelon and
the Selfborn, who is later called “God over all” (7.20).
   For apophatic discourse based on the Parmenides, see
Chapter 5. Such discourses were common in the second
and third centuries ce. Compare, for instance, Apuleius,
Plato and His Doctrine 1.5: God “is bodiless. He is one …
unmeasurable, the father and builder of all things, blessed
and provider of blessings, the very best, lacking nothing,
he himself bestowing all things. Indeed, Plato asserts that
he is heavenly, ineffable, unnamable, and as he himself
says, ‘invisible’ and ‘unconquerable’ (ἀδάμαστος).”24
   From Alcinous, we learn:

The primary God, then, is eternal, ineffable, “self-perfect” (that
is, deficient in no respect), “ever perfect” (that is, always per-
fect), and “all-perfect” (that is, perfect in all respects; divinity,
essentiality, truth, commensurability, good … He is the Good,
because he benefits all things according to their capacities, being
the cause of all good … he is Father through being the cause
of all things and bestowing order on the heavenly Intellect …
God is ineffable and graspable only by the intellect … he is not


22
     McCashen 2022, 627–28. 23 Alcinous, Handbook of Platonism 15.
24
     Cf. Apuleius, God of Socrates 3.4–6; cf. Plato, Republic 5.451a3.


                                  162
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation

a part of anything, nor is he in the position of being a whole
which has parts, nor is he the same as anything or different from
anything; for no attribute is proper to him, in virtue of which
he could be distinguished from other things. Also, he neither
moves anything, nor is he himself moved … God is partless, by
reason of the fact that there is nothing prior to him … From all
these considerations it becomes clear also that he is incorporeal.
(trans. Dillon 1993, 10.3–4).25

4.24–26 not infinite … not finite … not large, not small. The
double negation heightens the apophaticism, and the
technique is attested in Alcinous (see the preceding note).

4.28 It is entirely impossible to understand him. According to
Plato, Timaeus 28c, it is “difficult” to find the Father. In
the Stobaean Hermetica 1, it is difficult, but not impos-
sible to understand God. Gregory of Nazianzus insisted,
with SBJohn, that it is “impossible to understand” God.26

4.29 He is nothing of entities that exist … not as being supe-
rior. The One is beyond being, as is the Good in Plato’s
Republic 6.509b. The author(s) of SBJohn hesitated to
affirm the One’s superiority since that might imply that
there were others to measure up against.

4.30 He does not share in the eternal realms. The eternal
realms (or “aeons”) have both a spatial and a temporal
dimension. As a being outside time, the Invisible Spirit
does not decide when he will prepare or generate other
entities. A doctrine of eternal generation is assumed.
In this way, the author(s) of SBJohn could answer the

25
     The best discussion of negative theology among Nag Hammadi
     sources is Williams 2000, 277–302.
26
     Gregory, Orations 28.4 (Hermetic Fragment 38 in Litwa 2018, 231).


                                  163
     Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


perennial question: Does God create eternally or in time?
God eternally generates, while a lower being (Yaldabaoth)
creates the instruments of time (astral bodies).

4.37 Blessed one giving blessedness. Apuleius also described
God as “blessed and provider of blessings” (Plato and His
Doctrine 1.5). Likewise, the Good is a common Platonic des-
ignation (e.g., Plato, Republic 6.509b), but resonates with
GMark 10.18 and parallels: “No one is good, only God.”

5 The Emanation of Barbelon
5.1 He alone contemplates himself. The Father’s stream of
consciousness, flowing in all directions, circulates back-
ward. Because he is alone, there is nothing to contemplate
but himself. He is, in Aristotelian terms, thought think-
ing itself.27 Later Platonists adapted this idea. According
to Alcinous, the primary intellect “must be everlastingly
engaged in thinking of itself and its own thoughts.”28

5.2 living water. For the Johannine imagery, see Chapter
3; for possible allusions to baptism, see the Introduction.

5.3 He sees his own image taking every shape. For self-​
contemplation through water, compare the myth of
Narcissus.29 The power to take every shape is later mim-
icked by Yaldabaoth, who can change the shape of his
face or form at will (12.34). The Father is the formless
origin of all forms. Compare the Naassene discourse at
Refutation of All Heresies 5.7.33.


    27
         Aristotle, Metaphysics 12.9 (1074b33–34); Ref. 7.19.7.
    28
         Alcinous, Handbook of Platonism 10.3, trans. John Dillon.
    29
         Hadot 1976, 81–108.


                                 164
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


  The light water that surrounds him is not physical water
but metaphorically refers to the overspill of the Father’s
brilliant consciousness. Nevertheless, the image of waters
or the “deep” is probably informed by Gen 1.2 (“a spirit
of God hovered over the deep” [LXX]). Primeval water
(Gen 1.2) and light (1.3) could mix.

5.4 his thought became a thing. For Platonists, thought is
the true reality; thus, for divine beings, reality comes from
thought. The first and primal act of reproduction is asexual.

5.6 The exact meaning of Barbelon – as her name is
spelled in Codex III – is unknown. F. C. Burkitt sug-
gested a derivation from the Coptic belbile or “seed.”30
B. Layton proposed the Coptic berbir (“projectile,”
“emission”) or berber “boiling over, overflow.”31 H.
Leisegang traced it back to the Hebrew expression
bearba’ ’eloh “in the four is God” (a reference to the
tetragrammaton).32 G. Quispel suggested chabe r̄ baal,
“companion of the Lord.”33 M. Tardieu thought of bar-
bal (bālā), “the heart shone.”34 S. Pétrement preferred
barbe l̄ , which could mean “son of the Lord” or “son of
the Husband.“35 In doing so, she revived the old sug-
gestion of Isaac de Beausobre (1739), who suggested bar
baal, “son of the Lord.”36 We find “Barbarbelō cha” as a
divine name next to “Barbaiaō ch” in the Greek Magical
Papyri (PGM) 12.157. This being seems to be envisioned
as a serpent-faced god associated with the creator deity

     Burkitt 1932 54, 58–61. 31 Layton-Brakke 2021, 15, n.3.

     Leisegang, 1924, 186, suggested earlier by Harvey 1857, 1.221, n.2,

Ptah. We can also compare Helios Berbelō ch of PGM
5a.1, who is presumably male and associated with the
sun.37 Both spells are spells inducing a divine revelation.
Neither one explains the name of Barbelo(n). They do
indicate, however, that when Egyptians heard the name
“Barbelo(n),” they might have associated it with an epi-
thet of one of their native gods.
   The variation between Barbelon and Barbelo in other
versions of SBJohn does not seem semantically signifi-
cant. The final “n” is not necessarily a sign of the accusa-
tive or the Greek neuter.

5.8 Barbelon as first Thought is reminiscent of Helen in
Simonian theology. Simon was the primal Mind, and
Helen was his first Thought (ἡ πρώτη ἔννοια).38 Later
(6.6, 11), a separate being called “Thought” will act as an
attendant of Barbelo.

5.9 As triple male, Barbelon is the archetypal gender
bender. Since s/he is male–female, it is in fact arbitrary to
refer to her with stable feminine pronouns. She is later
called “paternal” (79.5).
   Barbelon as triple hymn or perhaps “triple hymned” is
distinctive to Codex III. All other versions call her the
“triple power” at this point, a title which SBJohn III
includes after the triple name.

6 Barbelon’s Four Attendants
6.1 Barbelon asked. Barbelon’s four attendants (the
Tetrad) emanate asexually by the consent of the Invisible
Spirit. The Mother must ask the Father for his consent in

37                          38
     Jackson 1989, 74–75.        Justin Martyr, 1 Apology 26.1–2.


                                     166
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


order to produce legitimate offspring. Even the heavenly
household is hierarchical.

6.11 When Barbelon is included with her offspring, they
make up a Pentad. Because the Pentad has male and female
aspects (we learn only the female names), a Decad arises
(6.12). For the Neopythagorean background, see Chapter 5.

7 The Selfborn God
7.1 Although Barbelon is not a material entity, her motion
is described in concrete, virtually sexual language. She
does not ask for the Father’s consent; this time, she looked
more intensely and revolved into the Father’s light.

7.2 The only spark mentioned in SBJohn is not a divine
spark in human beings, but the Selfborn.

7.4 The Onlyborn (familiar from GJohn 1.18) is also the
Selfborn (Autogenes). The pseudo-Orphic poem quoted by
Pseudo-Justin and Clement of Alexandria uses the word
αὐτογενής (“Selfborn”) to refer to the single God as source
of everything;39 it also appears in the preface of Sibylline
Oracles in reference to the God who rules all.40 The Kore
Kosmou refers to the chief creator as “selfborn.”41
   It seems remarkable that a term which seems to
describe an original or primary deity is assigned to a
subordinate figure,42 unless “Selfborn” rather highlights
non-sexual ­origin. According to Zostrianos, the Father,

     Εἷς ἔστ’ αὐτογενής, ἑνὸς ἔκγονα πάντα τέτυκται, Recension A, 1.8
     (Orphic Fragment 377 F Bernabé = 245.8 Kern).
40
     Αὐτογενὴς ἀγένητος ἅπανtα κρατῶν διὰ παντός, frag. 1.17 (Geffcken 228).

                                     167
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


Son, Geradamas, and the perfect man are all called “self-
born.”43 The Peratai also call their second principle
“selfborn.”44 According to S. Pétrement, “Selfborn” is
an inherently Christian epithet, because it assumes that
the Son is consubstantial with the Father. In effect, the
Son – who shares the Father’s essence – gives birth to
himself.45

7.5 The great Invisible Spirit rejoiced over the Light. What
makes the Selfborn different from Barbelon’s other ema-
nations is the Father’s rejoicing. In a strictly Platonic
treatment, the Father would not have emotions. The
Selfborn is in some way a special representative of the
Father, though he does not replace Barbelon as the divine
image. The author(s) of SBJohn were perhaps thinking
of Genesis 5.3, Seth born as the likeness of his father.

7.6–8 In order to ensure his perfection, the Father anointed
the Selfborn so that he became the Anointed Christ.
Goodness could also be understood as “Christhood,” but
the Selfborn is Christ (anointed) regardless of what he is
anointed with. Philo presented the Logos as son of God
and Wisdom, who was “anointed with oil,” which is, alle-
gorically, an “illumination by brilliant light.”46 Anointing
signifies the reception of knowledge as light.

7.10–16 Technically, the Selfborn only asked for one
attendant (Intellect), but he ends up with four (to balance
with Barbelon’s four attendants). It is the Invisible Spirit
who manifests the others: Will and Speech. Christ is not

43
     Van den Broek 1996, 56–66. 44 Ref. 5.12.3 (Litwa 2016, 276).
45
     Pétrement 1990, 418.
46
     Philo, On Flight and Finding 109–10. See further Tripaldi 2018, 113.


                                   168
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


Speech (as in λόγος, GJohn 1.1), but he uses it to construct
the higher realms. Christ is the true God and creator,
as opposed to Yaldabaoth who will construct the lower
realms. By means of his status as the (higher) creator,
Christ is God over all things (7.20), meaning God over
the upper (non-material) world. Compare Heracleon’s
Commentary on John, as reported by Origen.47

7.18 The Selfborn is called attendant (παράστασις) or
“righthand man”48 to the Invisible Spirit, and receives the
same sort of honor (cf. GJohn 5.23).

7.21 Truth is subject to Christ (note the heavenly hierar-
chy), but the author(s) take care not to identify Truth as
Christ’s spouse or partner.

7.22 For the hidden name of Christ given to the worthy,
compare Revelation 2.17; 3.12.

8 The Four Lights
8.1 In Pythagorean terminology, the four great Lights are
a Tetrad that gives rise to a Dodecad – a set of twelve
eternal beings.

8.2–3 {The three are Will, Eternal Life, and Thought.} His
four are Grace, Understanding, Perception, and Thinking. In
the present context, the three are out of place. Will and
Eternal Life were partners in the Barbelo treatise.49 The
author(s) of SBJohn preserved some of the paired names
and – apparently to avoid redundancy – replaced Willing
(Θέλησις) with Perception (Ἄισθησις). These changes may

47
     Origen, Comm. Io. 2.14.100. See further Wucherpfennig 2002, 104–59.
48
     LSJ s.v. παραστάτης II.3. 49 Irenaeus, AH 1.29.


                                   169
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


also go back to an earlier rewriting of the Barbelo treatise
before it became part of SBJohn.50
   Overall, however, the scheme of the four Lights with
their four partners (in the Barbelo treatise) is replaced
by the four Lights and their three respective attendants
(in SBJohn).51 Some of the names of the partners are
repeated among the attendants (Grace, Understanding,
and Perception). As for the other nine attendant names,
they were either invented or pulled from another source.
Some of them represent mental faculties (e.g., Perception,
Memory), while most indicate Christian virtues. For Grace
and Truth, see GJohn 1.17; for Love, see GJohn 13.34–38;
15.9–19; and for Peace, see GJohn 16.33; 20.19–21.52

8.4–12 In SBJohn, the eternal beings are not sexually gen-
erated. They are deferred aspects of Christ’s conscious-
ness, with Wisdom, as the twelfth eternity, under the
charge of the fourth Light, Eleleth. Primal reality can-
not lack or be completed (4.11). Only a deferred divinity
can become deficient. Wisdom was never anointed, like
Christ, so there was a limit to her knowledge.

8.15 son of the Selfborn. Apparently a reference to Adamas,
introduced in 9.2.

9 Denizens of the Four Realms
9.4 Adamas’ undefeatable intellectual power is comparable
to Metis, absorbed into Zeus according to Greek mythol-
ogy. But this power is not identified with Adam’s partner.
There is no heavenly Eve.

50
     Thomassen 2023, 132. 51 Thomassen 2023, 135.
52
     See further Thomassen 2023, 136, with n. 64.


                                170
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


10 The Story of Wisdom
10.2 Wisdom’s thought is defined more precisely as a senti-
ment (ἐνθύμησις). Z. Pleše takes ἐνθύμησις to refer to discur-
sive thought.53 More simply, it is thought arising from the
heart (θυμός), shot through with affect and desire.54
   Primal Knowledge is somehow involved in the sentiment
(ἐνθύμησις) of the spirit. The spirit is probably Wisdom’s
spirit, since the Invisible Spirit and Barbelon would not
experience a sentiment, as it is here defined.

10.3 Wisdom tried to manifest her likeness within her. She
wanted to manifest her own likeness, to reproduce her-
self, much like the Father produced Barbelon. Her inten-
tions were innocent.

10.4 Unlike Barbelon, Wisdom did not ask for male
consent to produce other powers. Technically, Wisdom
should not need a partner, because no one else does in the
upper world. Her partner is unnamed, but he is called her
consonance (10.6) and the spirit of virginal maleness (10.4).

10.8 her product emerged. Wisdom is like the Father, in that
her thought became a thing (5.4). She is not evil or lewd.55
(There was no one with whom to be lewd. She reproduced
Yaldabaoth asexually.) Rather, Wisdom plays the role of
transgressive daughter in a patriarchal household.

10.10 It did not have … the likeness of its mother. Here for the
first time, Wisdom is called “mother,” meaning “mother
of Yaldabaoth.” But Wisdom is also mother of the elect
(see 12.36; 13.1, 12, 15; 15.1, 4; 18.14; 19.20). One can

53
     Pleše 2006, 149. 54 Cf. Thomassen 2023, 61.
55
     Contrary to Logan 1996, 123.


                                 171
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


distinguish Barbelo as Mother (capital “M”) from wisdom as
mother (lowercase). Philo also called Wisdom “mother.”56

11 Yaldabaoth
11.1 Again, Wisdom is not foolish. In the midst of her
crisis, she is able to act with judgment. She notices that
Yaldabaoth does not resemble her. Ancient theories
ascribed formative power to the male semen.57 A mal-
formed child was thought to be insufficiently crafted by
the power of the father. This is why Hera’s fatherless
child Hephaestus was lame, and why her son Typhon was
a monster.58
   Despite being deformed, the “first ruler” (Yaldabaoth) is
still a distorted and deferred likeness of the Father, the singu-
lar ruler (5.2–3). Although Yaldabaoth is not alone, he claims
as much (12.51). He manages to assert power over a set of
subordinate beings who are mock images of the eternities.

11.5–6 Yaldabaoth. Debate over this name continues.
Scholars have proposed: “Yah, god of the fathers” (yah e l̄
da’baho t̄ h),59 “child of chaos” (yalad bao t̄ h),60 “god of long-
ings” (’el ta’aboth)61 “father of Sabaoth” (yalda ’abao t̄ h),62
“progenitor of shame,”63 “son of shame” (yalda behūta),64
or “producer of armies.“65 The name might simply meld

56
     Philo, That the Worse Attacks the Better 115; On Drunkenness 31;
     On the Confusion of Tongues 49.
57
     Aristotle, Generation of Animals 716a–b. Cf. Galen, Use of Parts 14.6;
     Ref. 6.30.6–9.
58
     Homeric Hymn to Apollo 349–55. See further Goehring 1981, 16–23.
59
     Harvey 1857, I.230, n.1. 60 Logan 1996, 126.
61
     Giversen 1963a, 200–1.

     Scholem 1974, 419–21; Giversen 1963a, 199–201; Logan 1996, 126–27.
63
     Ginzberg 1925, 5.138. 64 Black 1983, 69–72 at 71.
65
     Barc and Funk 2012, 246.


                                    172
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


different designations for the Jewish God: Yahweh, El
(perhaps also a shortened form of Adonai), and Sabaoth
(compare, e.g., Ps 89.9 LXX).66 At any rate, Yaldabaoth
is the Judean Lord. He is the first ruler only in his (lower)
world; he is not the God over all (7.20).

11.7–8 The great power that Yaldabaoth takes from his
mother recalls the Simonian designation “Great Power” for
God (Acts 8.10).67 Later it is said that Wisdom gave him
this power (15.1). Yaldabaoth shifting from place to place
resembles the hovering of Wisdom later in the story (13.1).

11.12–12.4 Yaldabaoth begins by creating a set of (astral)
authorities and angels that mimic the numeric patterns
seen among the eternities (though a pattern of twelve,
seven, and three does not exactly conform to the Tetrads,
Pentads, and Decads earlier). Like the Demiurge in Plato’s
Timaeus, Yaldabaoth uses a model for his creation, but he
is not directly conscious of it. Any connection he has to
the upper realms is mediated through the power taken
from his mother.

12 Yaldabaoth’s Creations
12.5–22 With the possible exception of Harmas (Hermes/
Mercury?), the names of the twelve authorities all appear
to be Greek renditions of Semitic names. A. J. Welburn
was able to correlate the names of the twelve with the
signs of the Zodiac.68


66
     Grant 1957, 148–49; Rasimus 2009, 103–5; Dan 1998, 311–21.
67
     Litwa 2024b, 44–45.
68
     The following charts are adapted from Welburn 1978, 244, 250. See
     also Logan 1996, 131.


                                  173
      Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation

                     Haoth                   Leo
                     Harmas                  Virgo
                     Galila                  Libra
                     Yobel                   Scorpio
                     Adonaios                Sagittarius
                     Sabaoth                 Capricorn
                     Kaïnan Kasin            Aquarius
                     Abiressia               Pisces
                     Yobel                   Aries
                     Armoupiael              Taurus
                     Adonin                  Gemini
                     Belias                  Cancer

   Yobel, for instance, is the transliterated Hebrew word for
ram, presumably designating Aries. Kaïnan Kasin is called
the sun. Mention of the sun in the seventh place fits the divi-
sion of twelve signs into seven day signs and five night signs.
The day signs rule heaven; the nights signs rule over the
abyss. (This point, incidentally, might explain why Yobel is
doubled – one rules in heaven, the other over the abyss.)
   Welburn’s theory, however, should not give the
impression that scholars have unlocked the mystery of
these names. There is continued disagreement about what
they mean and how exactly they correlate to the planets
and zodiacal signs.69 Problems are compounded when the
manuscripts of SBJohn do not agree on the names and
their order.
   What seems clear is as follows. Some of the names are titles
of the Yahweh in the Hebrew Bible. Adonaios and Adonin
appear to be forms of the same Hebrew name (the same root
as Adonis) meaning “Lord.” It frequently appears in magical

     69
          Tardieu 1984, 277–85 gives a completely different account.


                                   174
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


papyri (e.g., PGM 1.310; 4.1560, 1735, 12.264), and gem
amulets.70 Belial is a name for the devil (a variant of Beliar in
2 Cor 6.15), who also appears as a character in the Ascension
of Isaiah 1–6. The twelve and the seven are manifestations of
Yaldabaoth’s powers; and since Yaldabaoth is effectively the
devil, divine and demonic names could mingle.
   To the author(s) of SBJohn, it was important to use the
names which originated from acts of lust and rage (12.17)
because these (true) names weaken and disperse the rul-
ers (a sign that the names were used in exorcistic for-
mulas). In turn, the honorific names bestowed by Saklas
(Aramaic for “fool,” an alternative name for Yaldabaoth)
are false and add to the power of these demonic figures.

12.23–32 Then he ordered. The text might indicate that
Yaldabaoth divided the twelve into a set of seven (to rule
heaven as planetary powers) and a set of five (as powers in
control of the earth, aka chaos and the underworld). But the
names of the seven are different from the list of twelve
and the remaining five rulers are never named. The fol-
lowing chart is again adapted from Welburn:71
                    Aoth                    Moon
                    Eloaios                 Mercury
                    Astophaios              Venus
                    Yazo                    Sun
                    Adonaios                Mars
                    Adonin                  Jupiter
                    Sabbadaios              Saturn
  These names are again mostly variants of the Hebrew
names for God: Yahweh, Adonai, and Sabaoth. Astophaios

   70
        Jackson 1989, 75; Delatte and Derchain 1964, §§297, 485, 517.
   71
        Welburn 1978, 244–45.


                                  175
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


(aka Astaphaios) is an exception. The name appears, for
example, in the Greek Magical Papyri 12.288 as one of
the names invoked in the consecration of a ring, and by
the “Ophites” as recorded by Origen.72
  The seven are presumably in charge of the planets.
They have animal faces. These deities parody Egyptian
animal-​headed gods; the ass-faced archon in particular
recalls Seth-Typhon.73 But the polemic is also against
Jewish angelology which imagined angels (planetary pow-
ers) as in charge of the seven planets and the seven-day
week. The number seven does not appear in the upper
realm, the realm of eternities. It is a number only import-
ant for Yaldabaoth.

12.34 Yaldabaoth can manifest every one of the animal
faces of his children because he combines their powers.
He mimics the power of the Invisible Spirit who mani-
fests every form in the light water (5.3).

12.35–37 Yaldabaoth is superior to the other powers
not by nature, but by virtue of his mother’s pure light …
power. By virtue of this power, Yaldabaoth is called Lord –
a reverential name for Yahweh in the Hebrew Bible.
Yaldabaoth’s lust for power means that he will not share
it with others. He calls himself God above all, but the true
God above all is the Selfborn (7.20). Yaldabaoth and his
minions only have control of the lower (material) world.

12.37 Yaldabaoth is in rebellion against his real nature (ὑ
πόστασις). This word can also mean “origin,” but “sub-
stance” or “nature” is more common in early Christian


72                                  73
     Origen, Against Celsus 6.31.        Litwa 2021a, 18–31.


                                    176
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


literature.74 Yaldabaoth has a fiery nature which is not the
same as luminous spirit.

12.39–49 Yaldabaoth mimics the Selfborn in generating
powers by word (7.16). Sexual reproduction was previ-
ously unknown; Yaldabaoth invented it first in heaven. The
powers he yokes (that is, marries) to the seven authorities
mimic names of the eternities. Providence is properly a title
of Barbelon; Anointed (or Christ) is a name of the Selfborn;
Wisdom is the name of Yaldabaoth’s mother. Three of the
seven powers – Providence, Understanding, and Wisdom –
correspond to the eternities of the Son.75 Thus there con-
tinues to be correspondence – though broken and incom-
plete – between the upper and lower worlds. The realms of
the lower powers are in the likeness of the Incorruptibles.

12.50–51 For the biblical language of Yaldabaoth’s boast,
see Chapter 3 and the extensive treatment by S. Johnston
(2021). The boast scene is an attack on the Judean creator
to be sure, but it is also an attack on other Christians who
identify the creator with the true God.76

13 Wisdom’s Repentance
13.1–2 her lack. This section on the mother’s hovering is
taken from the Berlin codex. Not surprisingly, then, its
content does not entirely agree with SBJohn III. Before
the birth of Yaldabaoth, SBJohn III goes out of its way to
emphasize that Wisdom was complete (10.7).
   In BG, Wisdom’s partner is here called Fulfiller. The
reproving of her partner is here the sole sign of Wisdom’s

74
     LSJ s.v., B.I.5; B.III; Lampe 1961, s.v. I.b.4, 6.
75
     Barc and Funk 2012, 259. 76 Luttikhuizen 2006, 28.


                                 177
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


blame in all of SBJohn. The language of SBJohn III is
here is unknown.

13.4–10 Christ, what is this hovering? In this exegetical
exchange, Christ explains a puzzling word in the Greek
version of Genesis (ἐπεφέρετο) with words adapted from
the Coptic translation of it (ⲉⲥⲛⲁ ⲉⲥⲛⲏⲩ).77

13.12 he was saying regarding his mother that she alone
existed. This observation in the Berlin Codex does not
accord with the previous boast “apart from me there is no
one” (12.51) in the same codex.

13.14 He was taking pride in them. Yaldabaoth’s boast log-
ically follows here, as indicated by Irenaeus (AH 1.29.4).
The author(s) of SBJohn moved up the words of the boast
to 12.51 so that it could motivate Wisdom’s repentance.

13.16–18 She wept much. Wisdom’s weeping recalls
Valentinian tradition.78 As in Valentinian lore, it inspires
a joint petition for her among her siblings (the twelve
eternities).79

13.20–21 He poured down on her a holy Spirit. Wisdom’s
reception of Spirit models the reception of Spirit/
Luminous Insight to be received by Adam hereafter.
The descent of Wisdom’s partner seems to be taken
from Valentinian lore.80 But for the author(s) of SBJohn,
Wisdom’s partner is not a distinct eternity like Jesus,
but rather an impersonal portion of holy Spirit from the
Invisible Spirit.


77
     Timbie 2008, 35–46 at 46. 78 Irenaeus, AH 1.4.2.
79
     Irenaeus, AH 1.2.3. 80 Irenaeus, AH 1.2.3.


                                  178
      Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


13.24 Her partner raises Wisdom to the ninth sphere, a
sphere known in Hermetic texts (compare the Discourse
on the Eighth and Ninth, NHC VI,6). The ninth sphere
is apparently still part of this cosmos, but is higher than
the fiery eighth sphere of Yaldabaoth, who resides above
the seven spheres of the planetary gods. If the upper realm
is the tenth sphere, then we have a ten-tiered cosmos (as
in 2 Enoch).81

14 The Plan of Salvation Begins: Adam in the Image
14.1–2 The redemption and rectification of Wisdom
begins even before humans are created. The whole point
of humanity’s creation is to save Wisdom and to help her
rejoin the Immortals.
   In other texts, the voice that comes to Wisdom comes as
a rebuke to Yaldabaoth. Here, Yaldabaoth overhears the
voice echoing like a bat kol (an echo from the sky, familiar
from Rabbinic literature): “The Human exists and the Child
of the Human!” Originally, this Human may have referred
to Adamas, which makes sense of the name given to the
animate Adam (14.11). In the present version, however,
the Human is probably Providence/Barbelo (a point clar-
ified in the longer version of SBJohn), and her child is not
Seth, but Christ the Selfborn.

14.6–7 The image of God is specifically the imprint of the
image. The imprint indicates that the seven powers did not
directly see the image, but rather a deferred projection of it.
Unlike Plato’s Demiurge, they cannot look directly at the
model, but only at an imprint on the wavy screen of water.


                        81
                             Böttrich 2019.


                                179
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


Water as a reflective surface recalls (or mimics) the Invisible
Spirit who saw his Image (Barbelon) in the light water (5.3).

14.8–9 The mytheme of (human) creation by seven angels
is old, going as far back as Saturninus (early second century
ce).82 Using the imprint as a model, the seven angels built
an animate (or “soulish”) human. They use the qualities that
they have, qualities made of soul (cf. Gen 2.7; 1 Cor 15.45).

14.11 The authorities associate the human with light,
because the Human they saw in the waters is luminous,
a being of light. The double entendre of the Greek φῶς
(“light”) or φώς (“man”) depending on the accent) in
Genesis 1.3 is in the background.

14.13–19 The young gods in the Timaeus made the lower
soul as well as the body of the first humans.83 In SBJohn,
seven powers first build Adam’s animate bone, sinew,
flesh, marrow, blood, enamel, and hair. Some reordering
of the text has perhaps occurred, since marrow should be
in the first place, as the body’s foundation (14.12). Compare
Plato, Timaeus 73b–76e, in which we find the order mar-
row, bones, sinews, flesh, skin, hair, and nails.84
   Strictly speaking, only female powers make the ani-
mate human. But since these female powers are “yoked”
to the male planetary authorities, there is a connection
between the human soul and the planets. By implication,
each planetary power has special control over each part

82
     Irenaeus, AH 1.24.1. 83 Runia 1986, 242–49.
84
     Van den Broek 1981, 46. Van den Broek was able to coordinate these
     souls substances to individual planets: Moon – marrow; Mercury –
     bones; Venus – sinews; Sun – flesh; Mars – Blood; Jupiter – skin;
     Saturn – hair (ibid., 48).


                                   180
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


of the human soul – though some of the parts (brain,
intestines, etc.) are missing. On a general level, compare
Corpus Hermeticum 16.14. “They [daimones] reshape
our souls to their own ends, and they rouse them, lying in
ambush in our muscle and marrow, in veins and arteries,
in the brain itself, reaching to the very guts.”

14.20–23 The adornment of the human by the angelic swarm
recalls the adornment of Pandora by the gods in Hesiod’s
Works and Days 60–84 (cf. his Theogony 571–613): “All
the gods who have their homes on Olympus had given her
each a gift, to be a sorrow to men who eat bread” (Works
and Days 82, trans. Richard Lattimore). An even closer par-
allel is Stobaean Hermetica 23.27. As God spoke about cre-
ating humanity, the planetary rulers “commonly perceived
his intent for each god to provide, as each was capable,
something to those [humans] who would be born.”85

14.24 it remained limp there for a long time. Adam’s inability
to rise was traditional. It appears as early as Saturninus.86
Compare the Naassene Preacher: “This is the human …
whom Earth brought forth alone. He lay without breath,
without moving, without stirring – like a statue – as an
image of that Human above, Adamas.”87 The tradition
coincides with rabbinic tradition of Adam as a lifeless
mass (golem), based in part on Psalm 139.16: “Your eyes
beheld my unformed substance.”88

14.25 Here we learn that, although the seven planetary
angels created the basic elements of Adam’s animate body,
there were 360 angels who did the joint welding, as it were.

85
     Litwa 2018, 114. 86 Irenaeus, AH 1.24.1. 87 Ref. 5.7.6.
88
     Genesis Rabbah 14.8. See further Pearson 1990, 36–37.


                                 181
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


For the total of 360, Tardieu devises this scheme: “Each
name of the horoscope under the governance of 7 angels
makes 84 angels for the 12 names. Each of these angels
has 3 powers and 3 × 84 = 252. Then 12 + 84 + 252 = 348
to which one adds the seven celestial kings and the 5 over
Hades.”89
   Barc believes that this passage is a gloss because it does
not appear in the longer version and because it introduces
a contradiction by adding the number of 360 angels to the
seven powers.90 The 360 are evidently the attendants of
the seven (14.21).

15 Adam Receives the Light Power
15.1 Salvation means the retrieval of Wisdom’s power.
Yaldabaoth did not steal the power, the Mother had
given it to him by innocent conveyance (here reading, with
Funk, ϩⲉⲛ ⲟⲩⲡⲣⲟⲩⲛⲓⲕⲟⲛ [ⲛⲧⲉ ⲟⲩ]ⲃⲁⲗϩⲏⲧ).91 Compare
the Second Discourse of Great Seth (NHC VII 50.27–
29), where Wisdom is “an impetuous transmitter
(ⲟⲩⲡⲣⲟⲩⲛⲓⲕⲟⲥ) because of her naivety.”92
   Conveyance translates ⲡⲣⲟⲩⲛⲓⲕⲟⲛ, which need not mean
“sexual desire.”93 Anne Pasquier showed that in ancient
dictionaries and in Old Comedy, προύνικος designated
a porter who conveyed goods outside the market for a
wage.94 Such porters were associated with youth, haste,
and impetuosity, but the primary meaning of προύνικος was
taken from its supposed etymological origin – προφέρω, to

89
     Tardieu 1984, 286. 90 Barc and Funk 2012, 275.
91
     For the light power given, not stolen, see Williams 2017, 265–72.
92
     On Wisdom’s innocence in SBJohn and 2DiscSeth, among other
     texts, see Williams 2017, 253–90, esp. 259–63.
93
     Wisse and Waldstein 1995, 112. 94 Pasquier 1988, 47–66.


                                    182
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


bring forward.95 προύνικος may have been used to invest
Wisdom with the qualities of the Platonic-Pythagorean
Dyad, namely, mobility, volatility, and fluidity.96 Later
“Gnostic” tradition understood “Prounikos” to describe
Wisdom’s “idiosyncratic psychology, which exonerated
her of wilful misconduct.”97 In short, προύνικος does not
indicate a moral shortcoming (lewdness or sexual profli-
gacy). Wisdom was impetuous to convey her power. But
hers was not “an act of hybris and insubordination.”98
SBJohn III, at least, does not propose “a highly patholog-
ical representation of the divine,” nor does it incorporate
“error and sin directly into the divine world.”99

15.3–7 The four Lights (or archangels), together with
the Selfborn (the true “God over all”, 7.20), deprive
Yaldabaoth of Wisdom’s power by tricking him into
blowing it into Adam. This is the second step taken to
save Wisdom’s power. The rest of human history will
now become a dizzying tale of attack and counterat-
tack, with darkness trying to pin down the light and
vice versa:

• First act: Adam created according to the model of
  Providence/Barbelon.
  ◦ Result: Adam is left inert.
• Second act: Adam is infused with Spirit/light power.
  ◦ Counterpunch: Adam put in the material world (the
    dregs of matter).

95
     Despite her knowledge of Pasquier, Cerioni still foregrounds an
     Irenaean sense of prounikos ‘stressing incontinence and … Sophia’s
     inability to control her passions’ (2021, 71)

     Thomassen 2023, 35. 97 Thomassen 2023, 147.
98
     Luttikhuizen 2017, 282. 99 Cerioni 2021, 65.

     Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


• Third act: Adam is given Luminous Insight as helper.
  ◦ Counterpunch: Adam is put in a fleshly body which
     contains a counterfeit spirit.
• Fourth act: Adam’s thought is elevated by “the Thought
  of the preexisting Light.”
  ◦ Counterpunch: humanity placed in “Paradise” of
     delight/luxury and exposed to the tree of “life”
     (= death).
• Fifth act: Adam refuses to obey Yaldabaoth’s
  command(s).
  ◦ Counterpunch: Yaldabaoth throws a trance on Adam.
• Sixth act: Luminous Insight unveils Adam’s mind and
  the first couple eats from the tree of knowledge.
  ◦ Counterpunch: Yaldabaoth curses, exiles, and dark-
     ens the first couple. He rapes Eve and produces sex-
     ual desire in Adam.
• Seventh act: the Mother sends her own proper spirit.
  ◦ Counterpunch: Yaldabaoth sends his angels to have
     sex with human women; consequently, the coun-
     terfeit spirit spreads and the world is flooded with
     darkness.
• Final act: the Mother descends in the form of Christ,
  who reveals the truth to John.

16 Luminous Insight
16.1–9 Luminous Insight, as “life,” and Adam’s “helper” is
the spiritual Eve.100 Here she plays the part of the male
by penetrating Adam and arriving inside him. Her plan
to live inside Adam was made in order to escape detec-
tion; by extension, however, it makes Adam the passive

                  100
                        Marjanen 2016, 60–61.


                              184
      Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


partner in a sort of spiritual marriage. The instruction
of Insight foreshadows her role at the tree of knowledge.
Humanity is not created for itself; it is created as the ves-
sel of Wisdom, to help Wisdom fill her deficiency.

16.8 rulers. Philo is the first to describe the heavenly bod-
ies as “rulers” (ἄρχοντες) in Special Laws 1.13–14.101

16.12–21 The rulers cannot espy spiritual light, but they
still notice Adam’s superiority. Instead of being able to use
Adam’s light for their own purposes, they are threatened by
it. They cannot remove Adam’s light power or Luminous
Insight, but they can lead Adam to forget his spiritual nature
by first putting him in the material realm, and then by coat-
ing him in flesh. The rulers mix the four elements, using
the four winds (cf. Ezek 37.9) and fire, like blacksmiths who
make a vast commotion as they forge their product. Compare
the One resting in silence (4.44; cf. 7.13).
    The counterfeit spirit, along with dark matter and lust is
built into human flesh. This is a logical, if terrible, counter-
measure against Luminous Insight embedded within
Adam. Compare Paul, who perceived “another principle at
work in my bodily members” (Rom 7.23). According to the
Testament of Reuben, seven spirits of deceit are appointed
against humanity (2.1–2): The spirit of fornication seated
in the nature [sic] and in the senses, the spirit of insatia-
bleness in the belly, the spirit of fighting in the liver and
gall; the spirit of obsequiousness and chicanery, the spirit
of pride, spirit of lying, and the spirit of injustice (3.1–7).
    In SBJohn, the material body is variously named, first as
the shadow of death, then as a chain of amnesia, and the cave.

                       101
                             Runia 1986, 250.


                                 185
     Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


The shadow of death is a biblical image (Ps 23.4). The
flesh as the cause of amnesia is an Orphic and Pythagorean
notion – that souls entering bodies must first drink from
the river Lethe (Forgetfulness) and forget their (heavenly)
origin. The cave evokes Plato’s famous image of the cave
(Republic 7.514a–517a), though in Plato’s allegory, the
cave represents the cosmos.
   For equally strong rhetoric against the flesh, compare
Corpus Hermeticum 7.2: “But first you must rip off the
tunic that you wear, the garment of ignorance, the foun-
dation of vice, the bonds of corruption, the dark cage, the
living death, the sentient corpse, the portable tomb, the
resident thief, the one who hates through what he loves
and envies through what he hates” (trans. Copenhaver).

16.23 the Thought of the preexisting Light. Even in the flesh,
Luminous Insight – already in Adam – has the power to
elevate Adam’s thought. Adam and his offspring never
lose the light (as in Irenaeus, AH 1.30.8); it is only buried.

17 Paradise
17.1–3 The rulers strike back by exposing Adam to all
sorts of material temptations, temptations which are alle-
gorically represented by paradise, taken to mean “delight”
already in the Septuagint (Gen 3.23). In essence, living
in flesh exposes Adam to the perception of pleasure, and
Yaldabaoth uses pleasure as a distraction. Mentioned
here are the pleasures of beauty and food, pleasures which
promise to give life but actually produce error, poison, and
death.

17.4–9 The tree erroneously branded “life” by the rulers
is another name for their counterfeit spirit. The ointment of

                            186
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


evil contrasts with the ointment of goodness received by
Christ (7.6).102 Other parts of the tree are taken as alle-
gories for vices. Contrast Philo, for whom the tree of life
represents the central or main virtue (variously identified
as goodness or reverence).103

17.12–15 SBJohn explains why the rulers forbade Adam
from eating from the tree of the knowledge of good and
evil. That tree allegorically refers to Luminous Insight,
indicating that the knowledge of good and evil was
already in Adam.104 Insight’s fruits would be picked from
Adam’s mind, not from a literal tree.

17.16–18 The snake (Gen 3.1) is not a positive character in
Sethian Christian thought. Philo saw the snake as a sym-
bol of pleasure (Allegorical Interpretation 3.68). SBJohn
III sees the snake as a sign of the ultimate pleasure, sex,
which is defined as the defilement of destruction. Sex is
another tool of Yaldabaoth, who, not by ­accident, has the
face of a snake (11.1).
   Adam as useful to Yaldabaoth may have sexual connota-
tions. Yaldabaoth used Adam’s body for sexual pleasures
(a tacit polemic against pederasty). After Yaldabaoth
makes the material Eve, he will use her body in the same
way. At this point, Adam’s wisdom is manifest by his dis-
obedience to Yaldabaoth’s commands. Adam was already
“eating” from the tree of knowledge (that is, Luminous
Insight within him).

102
      Bream 1987, 122.
103
      Philo, Allegorical Interpretation 1.59; 3.52; Questions on Genesis
      1.9–10. See further Leonhardt-Balzer 2020, 236–48.
104
      Apuleius had named the knowledge of good and evil “prudence”
      (Plato and His Doctrine 2.6).

        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


18 The Creation of Eve
18.1–15 The reader is again reminded that Luminous Insight
abides within Adam. Yaldabaoth wished to remove her, but
she is unattainable. Compare GJohn 1.5: “Light shines in the
darkness, and the darkness has not grasped it.”105
   According to SBJohn III, Luminous Insight is never said
to leave Adam. Still united with him, she unveils his mind.
Consequently, Adam recognizes his joint essence resembling
him, which is apparently Luminous Insight, not the secondary
formation in the shape of a woman (that is, Eve, never named
in SBJohn III). The mother of the living is, again, probably
Luminous Insight (here called the mother’s partner), not Eve
specifically. The “kind of material Yaldabaoth uses for the
creation of Eve is not explained” in SBJohn III.106
   leave his father and mother. The father may refer to
Yaldabaoth and the mother to chaos.107 Chaos in SBJohn
is another name for matter.

18.16–17 The snake in the tree must be replaced
by a good animal, the eagle who soars with spiritual
insight. Biblically, the eagle acts as divine messenger in
Revelation 8.13 and is one of the four animals around
the throne of God (Rev 4.7).108 One can think here of
Greco–Roman gods turning themselves into birds,109 or
perhaps the emperor’s deified soul (since eating from the
tree of knowledge was an act of deification).110 The snake
­himself (truly) reported: “You shall be as gods, knowing


105
      Bream 1987, 127. 106 Marjanen 2016, 59. 107 King 2006, 105.
108
      Bream 1987, 129.
109
      Czachesz 2009, 162–82, citing Homer, Iliad 7.58–61; Odyssey
      22.239–40.

         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


good and evil” (Gen 3.5). Compare the Hermetic Prayer
of Thanksgiving: “You have made us gods by your gno-
sis” (NHC VI,7 64.18–19).
   The spiritual Eve (Luminous Insight) instructs the first
couple together, not Eve specifically (cf. Gen 3.5). It was
already said that Insight instructed Adam (54.13).

19 Cain and Abel
19.3 He added … “Your husband will rule you.” It is true that
Adam’s domination of Eve is a curse. Thus we can cer-
tainly understand this line as a criticism of the social status
quo.111 At the same time, refuting (violent) domination
over women – a kind of domination that Yaldabaoth will
soon illustrate – is not the same as undercutting hierarchi-
cal relationships. Long before Yaldabaoth’s curse, female
eternities are consistently portrayed as in submission to
males (and, ultimately, to the Father). Hierarchy and sub-
mission have already been naturalized in SBJohn.112
19.8–9 The earthly woman is seen attending Adam. A
subordinate relationship seems to be implied, like what
we see when the Son attends the Father, and the four


111
      King 2011, 535.
112
      Lillie (2017, 276) argues that Yaldabaoth’s rape ushers “in a hierarchy
      between male and female that is not part of the divine plan.” But
      hierarchy was already there (see notes on SBJohn 12.12; 18.16). She
      asserts (2017, 266) that Yaldabaoth’s curse “makes clear that the
      construct of male over female does not actually regulate the divine
      realm.” But the pattern of female subordination is rooted in the story
      of God, long before we come to the relation of Adam and Eve. It is
      absolutely true that there is no violence in the upper realm, but there
      is the subordination of female eternities to their male counterparts.
      Female aeons – even if they are male–female – were expected to show
      deference to the invisible Spirit and their male partners.


                                     189
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


intellectual faculties attend Barbelon (6.1–10). The fact
that Eve remains a virgin indicates that Adam did not seek
sex with her. Sex was not yet invented for humans.
   Yaldabaoth invented sex (19.15) and will soon sow sex-
ual desire into Adam (19.16). In context, sex seems to be
a specific manifestation of their counterfeit spirit (19.16).
Sex between Yaldabaoth and Eve is called defilement. The
defilement could refer to the fact that Yaldabaoth raped
Eve, but one could understand all sex, in SBJohn, as pol-
luting. Compare the “lustful procreation that defiles –
that is, destruction” (17.17).113
   Apart from the terrible violence of this passage, there is
perhaps a comic element in that Yaldabaoth is overcome
with lust for his own creation. The sexual escapades of
Zeus might lie in the background.114

19.10–17 Elohim is just, Yahweh unjust. For the tradition
of Yahweh and/or Satan having intercourse with Eve, see
Chapter 4. Philo distinguished two powers in heaven,
“God” and “Lord” – God being good, Lord being just.115
The split into a just god (Elohim) and an unjust god
(Yahweh) may reflect debates among Marcionite and
Valentinian Christians. Whereas Valentinian Christians
generally viewed the creator as just, Marcion, if not his
followers, viewed him as evil.116
   The author(s) of SBJohn solved the problem by dis-
tinguishing two figures to rule the earth, just and unjust.


113
      Tardieu 1984, 328. 114 Homer, Iliad 14.313–38.
115
      Philo, On the Life of Abraham 119–22; On the Change of Names
      28–29; On the Confusion of Tongues 137; On the Life of Moses
      2.99; On Dreams 1.162–63.
116
      Löhr 2002, 131–46.


                                 190
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


The just power is identified with Abel, who was called
righteous or just (Heb 11.4). The injustice of “Yahweh”
stems in part from his identification with Cain. In GJohn
8.44, 48, Yahweh is arguably the father of Cain, the liar
and murder (cf. 1 Jn 3.12).117 In SBJohn III, Yahweh is
the unjust Cain, though it is unclear exactly who Cain
represents, since the order of Abel and Cain is reversed in
other versions of SBJohn.
   The bear, like the lion, bespeaks ferocity (Hos 13.8;
Amos 5.19), power, and voracity (Dan 7.5).118
   19.18–19 In SBJohn III, Adam does not come to know
Eve specifically (as in Genesis 4.25) but his own lawless-
ness. Such lawlessness would appear to refer to Adam’s
newly implanted and unruly sexual impulse (19.16). The
fact that Adam fathered Seth according to (the model of) the
higher family might indicate some sort of asexual repro-
duction. That is to say, Adam’s intercourse was with the
spiritual Eve (= Luminous Insight) who came into Adam
just as Barbelon “revolved into” the Invisible Spirit to
produce Christ the Selfborn (7.1).

19.20–21 they sent to the mother some of her own proper
spirit. In the narrative, this is the third “injection” of
holy Spirit after (1) the mother’s spirit was blown into
Adam from Yaldabaoth and (2) Luminous Insight hid
in Adam. This triple shot of spirit (or one shot with two
“boosters”) was perhaps the inspiration of the Mother’s
three descents spoken of in the Providence hymn. All
three manifestations of the spirit are different avatars of
Wisdom.


117                         118
      Litwa 2021a, 40–53.         Bream 1987, 134.


                                     191
      Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


20 Souls Saved and Lost
20.1–11 Between the birth of Seth (Gen 4.25) and the
saving of Noah (Gen 6–8), the author(s) of SBJohn insert
this “little catechism,” featuring a dialogue between
Jesus and John primarily concerning the categories of
the saved. The four Lights have four model occupants:
Adamas, Seth, the Seed of Seth, and those who repent.
Interestingly, there is no attempt to map the types of
saved people onto these four levels. Salvation is con-
ceived of in general terms as the incorruptible gathering
or convocation of all the mother’s light into the Fullness.
  Jesus clarifies that those from the immovable family
have not only the Spirit of life, but also a set of virtues
which ensures their dominance over passions (rage, envy,
lust, greed). The extirpation of the passions is a kind of
purification.

20.12–19 Most of the redeemed cannot fully terminate
their passions, but they can at least hope to control … vice.
They also will be entirely and fully redeemed by virtue of
having the Spirit of life. Compare GJohn 6.63: “The Spirit
gives life.” It is sufficient for this type of soul to flee the
control of vice.
   The fact that power enters every person (20.16) indicates
that everyone has the light power of the mother to some
degree. There are no separate races of people who, empty
of spirit, can be dehumanized or condemned.
   As in Platonic ethics, it is assumed that no one chooses
to be evil or ignorant. According to Plato, humans want
the best for themselves, but they make poor decisions
because they misjudge what is good. In SBJohn, ignorant
people are ignorant because they are weighed down (20.25)


                             192
       Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


by the counterfeit spirit. People do not choose evil deeds.
They are dragged into them.

20.20–22 The human soul – here meaning the light power of
the Mother – is stronger than the counterfeit spirit. Those
who dominate the counterfeit rise to the upper realm.
Those still dominated are weighed down and given back to
the planetary authorities, who, as astral lords, distribute
them into new bodies. For these souls, recovering from
amnesia is the project of multiple lifetimes.119 The punish-
ment is remedial: The circle of transmigration lasts until
the person receives knowledge, becomes complete, and is
saved. Providence, aka the Spirit of life, works with people
through multiple lifetimes. When people follow and obey
the Spirit, they graduate from the cycle of transmigration.

20.34–35 The soul is assigned to another. Apparently a refer-
ence to a person’s guardian daimon.120

20.37–39 Despite the cruelty of the human situation, and
the sinister nature of creation, the author of SBJohn was
close to being a universalist. One must try hard to go to
hell by blaspheming against the Spirit (GMark 3.22–35).
Blasphemy against the Spirit is defined as rebellion against
the gnosis that Spirit gives. The angels of poverty are soon
to be introduced as those who have sex with women.

21 Fate and the Flood
21.1–15 When John asks whence the counterfeit spirit
comes, Jesus takes the opportunity to recommence his


 119
       Plato, Phaedrus 249a.
 120
       Plato, Laws 732c; 877a. See further Litwa 2021b, 45–56, 115–32.


                                  193
        Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


retelling of Genesis, skipping from Genesis 4 to 6 (to
the Flood story). It might seem, then, that the counter-
feit spirit comes into existence at the Deluge. In fact, this
spirit has already been introduced several times in the
narrative (e.g., 16.19; 17.6). For the author(s) of SBJohn,
the Flood narrative explains how the counterfeit spirit
spread like a virus among the human race.
   It is hard to avoid seeing a contradiction between the
statements that The first ruler knew that they surpassed him
in the height of their wisdom and he did not know that they
were wiser than him. It is another sign that the editor(s)
did not always harmonize their sources, probably because
they were not willing to fully rewrite them.
   Wiping out humankind with an apocalyptic disaster –
a flood – well describes the desperate plans of a threat-
ened tyrant. Yaldabaoth resembles a dictator whose last
act before overthrow is to detonate his entire nuclear
arsenal.
   Just as Providence warns Noah, Prometheus warns
Deucalion and urges him to construct an ark.121
   The author(s) of SBJohn rewrites the Noah story such
that the ark is unnecessary. The flood is allegorized: It
does not consist of water, but of darkness (i.e., ignorance).
The ark is not a boat, but something more like the ark
of the covenant, a sacred place protected by a cloud of light
(21.13).122

21.16–26 Instead of putting the “daughters of men”
episode before the Flood, as in Genesis 6, SBJohn puts
it after. The sexualization of the angelic invasion best

121                      122
      Meyer 2014, 227.         Rasimus 2022, 301–26.


                                    194
      Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


resembles 1 Enoch 6–10. According to 1 Enoch, the
“sons of god” are wicked angels who entice women by
a mix of arcane science and baubles. It is the semen of
these angels, apparently, that contains the counterfeit
spirit. (Semen was widely thought to contain πνεῦμα).123
Accordingly, the counterfeit spirit is literally injected into
these women like a virus. It then distracts, controls, and
hardens them, as well as their children. These children are
not automatically damned, but their stubbornness makes
it hard for these humans to be saved in a single lifetime.
   Between the Flood and Christ’s coming, humans are
kept from remembering their Providence (aka Barbelon).
But when Christ comes as a manifestation of Barbelon,
he reveals the knowledge which dissolves the ignorance
of the counterfeit spirit.

22 Conclusion
22.1 The adjective paternal points not to Wisdom, but to
the higher Mother Barbelon. In SBJohn III, it is never
explained how Barbelon takes shape among her children.
It would apparently refer to the three infusions of Spirit
into human beings. But if the Mother only came once before
(22.5), that would apparently refer solely to her manifesta-
tion as Insight. Her final descent is her revelation in Christ.

22.2–4 I rose up to the perfect realm. This is a description
of Jesus’ previous ascent (GLuke 24.51; Acts 1.9) and his
command to John to write the contents of his revelation
so that he can secretly deliver the writing (evidently the
text of SBJohn itself) to his fellow spirits. The secrecy


         123
               E.g., Aristotle, Generation of Animals 2.2 736a1.


                                   195
         Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation


heightens the perceived value of the revelation and the
sense of significance among those who receive it.124 The
fellow spirits are not necessarily the other apostles, but any
gnostic readers of SBJohn. The book contains the mystery
of the immovable family (22.4). Jesus himself guarantees the
accuracy of John’s writing.

22.9–10 The curse is not malignant, but serves as a warn-
ing spoken against those who prefer bodily goods to spir-
itual truth. Such protective orders were traditional ways
for scribes to rein in other scribes. Compare Revelation
22.18–19:

I warn everyone who hears the words of the prophecy of this
book: if anyone adds to them, God will add to that person the
plagues described in this book; if anyone takes away from the
words of the book of this prophecy, God will take away that
person’s share in the tree of life and in the holy city, which are
described in this book.

  Similar also is the oath that Hermes urges his disciple
to write at the end of the Discourse on the Eighth and
Ninth:

I make him who will read this holy book swear by heaven,
earth, fire, water, seven rulers of substance, the creative spirit
within them, by God of …, and the selfborn one, and him who
has been born, that he will guard what things Hermes has said.
And those who keep the oath, God will be reconciled with

124
      See further King 2012, 61–86. In my view, the secrecy motif does
      not serve to keep the book away from the wrong hands (contrary
      to Marjanen 2012, 93). The opponents of SBJohn, I think, would
      have been just as attracted to this secret book as its supporters (for
      obviously different reasons). Heresiologists sometimes gleefully
      exposed the “secrets” of their opponents.


                                      196
     Part IV Additional Notes to Text and Translation

them … But rage will come to each one of those who trans-
gress the oath (NHC VI,6 63,16–31).

  Codex III adds that people are cursed for exchanging
copies of SBJohn for silver and gold, possibly alluding to
the gifts of the angels (22.9).125

22.11–12 He began to speak. John is depicted as handing
over the mystery orally. The reader, of course, knows
that the contents of the mystery have been written down,
but the oral presentation here gives the impression that
reading SBJohn is the equivalent of hearing it from the
lips of the apostle himself.
